Landscaping and Grounds English

Clarify grounds-service requests, property access, appearance preferences, weather changes, material discrepancies, and unfinished-work handoffs.

For Landscape crew members, groundskeepers, lawn-service workers, and grounds assistants.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Explain the current work order, acknowledge an additional request, and refer it without inventing a price or appointment.
  • Separate a work-plan limit from an unverified ownership claim and refer an access request without deciding a legal boundary.
  • Resolve an ambiguous plant reference, describe the requested appearance, and preserve the qualified lead review.
  • Explain a work pause, preserve the customer deadline, and distinguish a requested update from a confirmed return visit.
  • Report a visible condition with its location and time, and respond to a suspected cause without presenting it as confirmed.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Confirming the booked grounds work02 Clarifying access and property limits03 Clarifying appearance and plant requests04 Explaining a weather-related change05 Reporting site observations without diagnosis06 Checking materials and requested quantities07 Responding to finish and cleanup concerns08 Handing over unfinished grounds work
Prompts

All sites, customers, plants, materials, times, work orders, and decisions in the cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not horticultural diagnosis, pruning technique, equipment operation, chemical application, land surveying, or legal boundary advice. Follow actual site controls, training, work orders, local requirements, and qualified-lead decisions. The dialogues do not authorize entering disputed land, pruning a plant, changing irrigation, applying products, substituting materials, or resuming paused work.

01Confirming the booked grounds workA hedge request during a lawn visit+

Your goal

Explain the current work order, acknowledge an additional request, and refer it without inventing a price or appointment.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

A hedge request during a lawn visit

At Oak Court, worker Asha explains the booked work to resident Ben. Today includes front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance. Ben asks the crew to shape the rear hedge as well. Hedge work is excluded from this booking. The office can review a separate request, but no price, date, or approval is confirmed. Asha must preserve the existing work scope while passing on the hedge request accurately, without implying that a quick favor is automatically covered.

Ben: Resident / Asha: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is included today?

Answer and explanation

D. Front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance

The booking names front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance as the included work.

2. Which task does Ben add?

Answer and explanation

C. Rear-hedge shaping

Ben requests shaping of the rear hedge beyond the current booking.

3. What can Asha offer?

Answer and explanation

D. Office review of a separate request, with no confirmed price or date

The office can review the additional request, but its terms and timing are not confirmed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The current booking includes front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance, not rear-hedge shaping. Referring Ben to the office preserves the additional request but does not approve it, price it, or book it for another day.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
work order
Record specifying the tasks assigned for a particular job.check the work order
booked scope
Work included in the agreed booking.confirm the booked scope
grounds maintenance
Upkeep of outdoor areas and landscape features.discuss grounds maintenance
front lawn
Grassed area at the front of the property.identify the front lawn
mowing
Cutting grass as part of the specified lawn task.confirm the mowing task
path
Defined walking route through an outdoor area.identify the path
leaf clearance
Removal of fallen leaves from the specified area.confirm path leaf clearance
rear hedge
Row of shrubs at the back of the site.identify the rear hedge
hedge shaping
Work intended to change or maintain a hedge's form.request hedge shaping
included task
Activity explicitly covered by the booking.name the included tasks
excluded task
Activity outside the current agreed work.explain the excluded task
additional request
New work requested beyond the existing arrangement.record an additional request
separate quotation
Price proposal for work outside the current booking.request a separate quotation
site visit
Attendance at a location for defined work or assessment.confirm the site visit
crew
Group of workers assigned to the job.brief the crew
grounds lead
Person supervising or assessing grounds work.consult the grounds lead
office review
Assessment of a request by the coordinating office.refer for office review
scope change
Amendment to the agreed work content.seek approval for a scope change
task authorization
Permission to carry out the specified work.confirm task authorization
service date
Day on which agreed work is scheduled.confirm the service date
price estimate
Provisional cost figure for specified work.distinguish a price estimate
confirmed booking
Arrangement explicitly agreed and entered through the actual process.verify a confirmed booking
request handoff
Transfer of a new request to the responsible person.complete a request handoff
completion claim
Statement that assigned work has been finished.avoid an unsupported completion claim

A task can be outside the booking even when the crew is already on site. Do not infer that it is free, quick, technically appropriate, or authorized. The conversation clarifies scope; it does not establish that the booked tasks have been completed.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the request before explaining the limit

A resident may see the crew on site and assume an additional task is easy to include. Explain the current work order in concrete terms, then identify the separate review route. A clear boundary can remain helpful without criticizing the resident for asking.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the request
I understand you would like the rear hedge shaped as well.
Name the booking
Let me confirm today's work order.
State the first task
Front-lawn mowing is included.
State the second task
Path leaf clearance is included too.
Name the exclusion
Rear-hedge work is outside this booking.
Keep the request specific
You are asking for the rear hedge, not the front lawn.
Explain the review route
The office can review a separate request.
Avoid a price promise
I do not have a confirmed price for that work.
Avoid a date promise
No service date is confirmed for the hedge.
Avoid an informal addition
I cannot add it as a quick favor to today's scope.
Ask permission to relay
Would you like me to pass the hedge request to the office?
Separate review and approval
A review request is not task authorization.
Preserve the existing work
Today's listed tasks remain mowing and path leaf clearance.
Avoid a universal refusal
I am explaining this booking, not ruling out future hedge work.
Keep completion separate
Confirming the scope does not mean the work is finished.
Close with the next step
I will refer the rear-hedge request with price and timing still open.
6 language and precision notes
As well
Adds a new request and does not prove it belongs to the original booking.
Included versus excluded
Describes the current agreed scope, not all services the business offers.
Separate
Keeps the new hedge request distinct from the existing lawn visit.
Can review
Offers assessment without promising acceptance or availability.
No confirmed date
Avoids turning a possible future service into a scheduled visit.
Quick favor
Informal wording that can obscure authority, cost, and task suitability.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which scope explanation matches the booking?

Answer and explanation

D. Today covers front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance, not the rear hedge.

The work order includes the two named front-area tasks and excludes hedge work.

2. Which next-step statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. I can pass the separate hedge request to the office for review.

Referring the request is available, while approval, cost, and timing remain open.

3. Which statement overpromises?

Answer and explanation

B. The hedge will definitely be shaped today at no charge.

Neither same-day service nor a free price is supplied or authorized.

4. What should remain in the handoff?

Answer and explanation

D. Rear-hedge shaping requested; separate review needed; price and date unknown.

The handoff must preserve the actual request and the unresolved approval, price, and timing.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

A hedge request during a lawn visit

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Resident / Asha: Grounds worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • booked scope
  • included task
  • scope change
  • separate quotation
  • Office review
  • request handoff
  • work order
  • task authorization
  • service date
  • rear hedge
  1. Ben
    Could you shape the rear hedge while you're here? I'd assumed the grounds visit covered that as well as the lawn.
  2. Asha
    I can pass that request on. Today's 1 lists front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance; the hedge isn't included.
  3. Ben
    Oh, I thought it was a general garden visit. You're saying the lawn and the path are the actual tasks on the booking?
  4. Asha
    That's the 2, yes. Being at Oak Court doesn't mean the crew has approval for every outdoor job.
  5. Ben
    Please tell the office it's the hedge at the back. I'm not asking for shaping around the front lawn.
  6. Asha
    I'll specify 3 shaping, so the location doesn't get confused with the mowing area. The office can review it separately.
  7. Ben
    Would that mean an extra bill? I don't want a request for information to be taken as agreement to a price.
  8. Asha
    No 4 is confirmed. Asking for a review doesn't commit you to a charge, and I don't have a price to offer.
  9. Ben
    All right. Could it happen later this week, or do they need to check the schedule before you can say?
  10. Asha
    The 5 still needs checking. I can relay your request, but I can't confirm a hedge appointment for this week.
  11. Ben
    Please don't tell the other residents it's booked yet. They'll expect someone to turn up if they hear a definite day.
  12. Asha
    6 is the next step. I'll keep it described as a request until there is an actual arrangement.
  13. Ben
    What if I only want a quick tidy? Is that different from asking for the hedge to be shaped?
  14. Asha
    The wording doesn't replace 7. We need the actual work assessed and agreed; I can't add it as a quick favor.
  15. Ben
    Then leave it as rear-hedge shaping. I'd rather the office knows what I'm asking for than gets an unclear note.
  16. Asha
    I'll make that 8 with the location and requested work. Today's mowing and leaf-clearance tasks stay as they are.
  17. Ben
    Can you repeat the two tasks before I speak to the residents? I don't want to say the hedge was missed.
  18. Asha
    Mowing is the 9 for the front lawn; path leaf clearance is also listed. The rear hedge was not part of today's booking.
  19. Ben
    Thanks. Please refer the hedge separately. I'll wait for the office to discuss the price and a possible day.
  20. Asha
    Yes. No 10 has been approved in this conversation. I'll send the request with both price and timing still open.

Answers and explanations
  1. work order Turn 2. Work order specifies today's front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance, rather than every grounds task.
  2. booked scope Turn 4. Booked scope limits the current visit to the agreed tasks; sharing a property does not add hedge work.
  3. rear hedge Turn 6. Rear hedge identifies the exact subject of the additional request and keeps it separate from the front-lawn task.
  4. separate quotation Turn 8. Separate quotation would provide a price for the additional work; none has been given or accepted here.
  5. service date Turn 10. Service date for the hedge remains unconfirmed and must not be presented as an appointment this week.
  6. Office review Turn 12. Office review assesses the new request and is separate from approval or an entered booking.
  7. task authorization Turn 14. Task authorization is still needed for the actual hedge work; changing the informal description does not add approval.
  8. request handoff Turn 16. Request handoff transfers the accurately described hedge request while leaving approval, price, and date unresolved.
  9. included task Turn 18. Included task identifies front-lawn mowing, alongside path leaf clearance, as work actually named for today's visit.
  10. scope change Turn 20. No scope change is approved; the separate hedge request is only being passed for review.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Could you shape the rear hedge while you're here? I'd assumed the grounds visit covered that as well as the lawn.
  2. Asha: I can pass that request on. Today's work order lists front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance; the hedge isn't included.
  3. Ben: Oh, I thought it was a general garden visit. You're saying the lawn and the path are the actual tasks on the booking?
  4. Asha: That's the booked scope, yes. Being at Oak Court doesn't mean the crew has approval for every outdoor job.
  5. Ben: Please tell the office it's the hedge at the back. I'm not asking for shaping around the front lawn.
  6. Asha: I'll specify rear hedge shaping, so the location doesn't get confused with the mowing area. The office can review it separately.
  7. Ben: Would that mean an extra bill? I don't want a request for information to be taken as agreement to a price.
  8. Asha: No separate quotation is confirmed. Asking for a review doesn't commit you to a charge, and I don't have a price to offer.
  9. Ben: All right. Could it happen later this week, or do they need to check the schedule before you can say?
  10. Asha: The service date still needs checking. I can relay your request, but I can't confirm a hedge appointment for this week.
  11. Ben: Please don't tell the other residents it's booked yet. They'll expect someone to turn up if they hear a definite day.
  12. Asha: Office review is the next step. I'll keep it described as a request until there is an actual arrangement.
  13. Ben: What if I only want a quick tidy? Is that different from asking for the hedge to be shaped?
  14. Asha: The wording doesn't replace task authorization. We need the actual work assessed and agreed; I can't add it as a quick favor.
  15. Ben: Then leave it as rear-hedge shaping. I'd rather the office knows what I'm asking for than gets an unclear note.
  16. Asha: I'll make that request handoff with the location and requested work. Today's mowing and leaf-clearance tasks stay as they are.
  17. Ben: Can you repeat the two tasks before I speak to the residents? I don't want to say the hedge was missed.
  18. Asha: Mowing is the included task for the front lawn; path leaf clearance is also listed. The rear hedge was not part of today's booking.
  19. Ben: Thanks. Please refer the hedge separately. I'll wait for the office to discuss the price and a possible day.
  20. Asha: Yes. No scope change has been approved in this conversation. I'll send the request with both price and timing still open.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The rear hedge is a new request

At Oak Court, a worker checks the resident's expectation against today's work order.

  1. Resident: Could you shape the rear hedge during today's grounds visit?
  2. Worker: Today's work order covers the front lawn and leaf clearance from the path, not hedge shaping.
  3. Resident: I thought our recurring service meant the whole garden was included on every visit.
  4. Worker: The recurring schedule lists particular tasks. I can ask the office to review your hedge request separately.
  5. Resident: Would asking them now guarantee that your crew can finish it during this visit?
  6. Worker: No. The scope, availability, and any price need confirmation before we describe it as booked.
  7. Resident: Please query the rear hedge, not the front shrubs.
  8. Worker: I'll name that location clearly and keep it separate from the work already listed today.
  9. Resident: Thank you. Can the office contact me with the actual options rather than a general estimate?
  10. Worker: I'll request office contact without promising extra work or timing.
The annual task is not every visit

A grounds worker clarifies a customer's seasonal-service expectation.

  1. Customer: Why isn't seasonal bed review listed on this week's work order?
  2. Worker: The agreement covers several visits, but this work order lists lawn care and front-path clearance only.
  3. Customer: Does that mean the seasonal review has been removed from my service agreement altogether?
  4. Worker: I don't have evidence of that. A task absent from today's list isn't necessarily canceled from the agreement.
  5. Customer: Can you check which visit it belongs to so I don't keep asking every crew?
  6. Worker: I can ask the office to confirm its scheduled visit and let you know the current status.
  7. Customer: Please specify front beds; another company handles our rear beds.
  8. Worker: I'll include that limit. We shouldn't combine two different service areas under a vague garden review.
  9. Customer: Then today's crew completes its listed work, while the office confirms the seasonal appointment separately.
  10. Worker: Today's work remains as listed; the office will clarify the seasonal task.
A request from someone who is not the contact

A worker checks a tenant's extra-work request at an office property.

  1. Tenant: Could you add the side courtyard? Our visitors arrive later.
  2. Worker: The work order names the main entrance only. Who coordinates grounds requests for your building?
  3. Tenant: Mara in facilities is the site contact, but I'm the tenant using the courtyard today.
  4. Worker: Thanks. I can relay your request to Mara rather than treat it as a change already approved.
  5. Tenant: Please explain the visitor timing, because otherwise she might think I'm asking about next month.
  6. Worker: I will. What time are visitors expected, and which courtyard entrance are they using?
  7. Tenant: Two o'clock at the east door; the rear parking area is excluded.
  8. Worker: I'll state the side courtyard and east door, with the two-o'clock event as your constraint.
  9. Tenant: That describes it accurately. I understand you can't promise the additional work on your own.
  10. Worker: The main-entrance booking stays unchanged while Mara reviews your courtyard request.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A hedge request during a lawn visit
  1. Read the included tasks: front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance.
  2. Repeat Ben's rear-hedge shaping request and the separate office-review route.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Refer another additional task

Elm Court has booked front-lawn mowing and driveway leaf clearance. A resident requests side-hedge shaping. It is excluded from the current booking; the office can review it, with no price or date confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The booked lawn task is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. mowing

Mowing is the included lawn task in the existing work order.

2. Resident: "The additional request concerns the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. side hedge

Side hedge is the subject of the separate shaping request.

3. Worker: "The next step is office ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review

Review is the available process for assessing the additional task.

4. Resident: "The price and date are still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unconfirmed

No quotation or service appointment has been established for the extra work.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming the booked grounds work
Original fictional case: At Oak Court, worker Asha explains the booked work to resident Ben. Today includes front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance. Ben asks the crew to shape the rear hedge as well. Hedge work is excluded from this booking. The office can review a separate request, but no price, date, or approval is confirmed. Asha must preserve the existing work scope while passing on the hedge request accurately, without implying that a quick favor is automatically covered.
Language workshop: A hedge request during a lawn visit
Communication goal: Explain the current work order, acknowledge an additional request, and refer it without inventing a price or appointment.
Language guidance: The current booking includes front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance, not rear-hedge shaping. Referring Ben to the office preserves the additional request but does not approve it, price it, or book it for another day.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you would like the rear hedge shaped as well. / Let me confirm today's work order. / Front-lawn mowing is included. / Path leaf clearance is included too. / Rear-hedge work is outside this booking. / You are asking for the rear hedge, not the front lawn. / The office can review a separate request. / I do not have a confirmed price for that work. / No service date is confirmed for the hedge. / I cannot add it as a quick favor to today's scope. / Would you like me to pass the hedge request to the office? / A review request is not task authorization. / Today's listed tasks remain mowing and path leaf clearance. / I am explaining this booking, not ruling out future hedge work. / Confirming the scope does not mean the work is finished. / I will refer the rear-hedge request with price and timing still open.
Vocabulary: work order: Record specifying the tasks assigned for a particular job. / booked scope: Work included in the agreed booking. / grounds maintenance: Upkeep of outdoor areas and landscape features. / front lawn: Grassed area at the front of the property. / mowing: Cutting grass as part of the specified lawn task. / path: Defined walking route through an outdoor area. / leaf clearance: Removal of fallen leaves from the specified area. / rear hedge: Row of shrubs at the back of the site. / hedge shaping: Work intended to change or maintain a hedge's form. / included task: Activity explicitly covered by the booking. / excluded task: Activity outside the current agreed work. / additional request: New work requested beyond the existing arrangement. / separate quotation: Price proposal for work outside the current booking. / site visit: Attendance at a location for defined work or assessment. / crew: Group of workers assigned to the job. / grounds lead: Person supervising or assessing grounds work. / office review: Assessment of a request by the coordinating office. / scope change: Amendment to the agreed work content. / task authorization: Permission to carry out the specified work. / service date: Day on which agreed work is scheduled. / price estimate: Provisional cost figure for specified work. / confirmed booking: Arrangement explicitly agreed and entered through the actual process. / request handoff: Transfer of a new request to the responsible person. / completion claim: Statement that assigned work has been finished.
Speaking task: Read the included tasks: front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance. Repeat Ben's rear-hedge shaping request and the separate office-review route. Read the price and date answers without adding a quotation or appointment.
Partner: Ben: Resident / Asha: Grounds worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Elm Court has booked front-lawn mowing and driveway leaf clearance. A resident requests side-hedge shaping. It is excluded from the current booking; the office can review it, with no price or date confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Can you repeat the two tasks before I speak to the residents? I don't want to say the hedge was missed.
Asha: Mowing is the included task for the front lawn; path leaf clearance is also listed. The rear hedge was not part of today's booking.
Ben: Thanks. Please refer the hedge separately. I'll wait for the office to discuss the price and a possible day.
Asha: Yes. No scope change has been approved in this conversation. I'll send the request with both price and timing still open.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Language workshop: A hedge request during a lawn visit
Communication goal: Explain the current work order, acknowledge an additional request, and refer it without inventing a price or appointment.
Language guidance: The current booking includes front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance, not rear-hedge shaping. Referring Ben to the office preserves the additional request but does not approve it, price it, or book it for another day.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you would like the rear hedge shaped as well. / Let me confirm today's work order. / Front-lawn mowing is included. / Path leaf clearance is included too. / Rear-hedge work is outside this booking. / You are asking for the rear hedge, not the front lawn. / The office can review a separate request. / I do not have a confirmed price for that work. / No service date is confirmed for the hedge. / I cannot add it as a quick favor to today's scope. / Would you like me to pass the hedge request to the office? / A review request is not task authorization. / Today's listed tasks remain mowing and path leaf clearance. / I am explaining this booking, not ruling out future hedge work. / Confirming the scope does not mean the work is finished. / I will refer the rear-hedge request with price and timing still open.
Vocabulary: work order: Record specifying the tasks assigned for a particular job. / booked scope: Work included in the agreed booking. / grounds maintenance: Upkeep of outdoor areas and landscape features. / front lawn: Grassed area at the front of the property. / mowing: Cutting grass as part of the specified lawn task. / path: Defined walking route through an outdoor area. / leaf clearance: Removal of fallen leaves from the specified area. / rear hedge: Row of shrubs at the back of the site. / hedge shaping: Work intended to change or maintain a hedge's form. / included task: Activity explicitly covered by the booking. / excluded task: Activity outside the current agreed work. / additional request: New work requested beyond the existing arrangement. / separate quotation: Price proposal for work outside the current booking. / site visit: Attendance at a location for defined work or assessment. / crew: Group of workers assigned to the job. / grounds lead: Person supervising or assessing grounds work. / office review: Assessment of a request by the coordinating office. / scope change: Amendment to the agreed work content. / task authorization: Permission to carry out the specified work. / service date: Day on which agreed work is scheduled. / price estimate: Provisional cost figure for specified work. / confirmed booking: Arrangement explicitly agreed and entered through the actual process. / request handoff: Transfer of a new request to the responsible person. / completion claim: Statement that assigned work has been finished.
Speaking task: Read the included tasks: front-lawn mowing and path leaf clearance. Repeat Ben's rear-hedge shaping request and the separate office-review route. Read the price and date answers without adding a quotation or appointment.
Partner: Ben: Resident / Asha: Grounds worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Elm Court has booked front-lawn mowing and driveway leaf clearance. A resident requests side-hedge shaping. It is excluded from the current booking; the office can review it, with no price or date confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Can you repeat the two tasks before I speak to the residents? I don't want to say the hedge was missed.
Asha: Mowing is the included task for the front lawn; path leaf clearance is also listed. The rear hedge was not part of today's booking.
Ben: Thanks. Please refer the hedge separately. I'll wait for the office to discuss the price and a possible day.
Asha: Yes. No scope change has been approved in this conversation. I'll send the request with both price and timing still open.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

02Clarifying access and property limitsThe plan stops at the fence+

Your goal

Separate a work-plan limit from an unverified ownership claim and refer an access request without deciding a legal boundary.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The plan stops at the fence

At Elm View, customer Rosa asks grounds worker Kai to clear a strip beyond the rear fence. Rosa says the strip is probably part of the property, but ownership is unconfirmed. The current work plan ends at the fence. Kai can report the additional request and uncertainty to the office but cannot determine the property boundary, extend the work area, or assume permission to enter. No ownership conclusion, clearance work beyond the fence, or revised plan is established.

Rosa: Customer / Kai: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Where does the current work plan end?

Answer and explanation

A. At the rear fence

The supplied work plan ends at the rear fence and has not been extended.

2. What is known about the strip's ownership?

Answer and explanation

D. It is unconfirmed

Rosa says probably, which does not establish who owns the strip.

3. What can Kai do?

Answer and explanation

B. Flag the request and uncertainty to the office

Kai can refer the issue but has no authority to determine ownership or expand the work area.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The work-plan limit is the rear fence. That fact does not prove the legal property line or ownership of the strip beyond it. Rosa says probably; the uncertainty must remain explicit in the office referral.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
work-plan limit
Endpoint of the area covered by the current assigned work.confirm the work-plan limit
rear fence
Fence at the back of the site used as a location reference.identify the rear fence
boundary
Line separating areas or property interests, requiring appropriate verification.avoid deciding the boundary
property line
Legally relevant limit of a property, not established here.request property-line clarification
ownership claim
Statement that land belongs to someone.qualify the ownership claim
unconfirmed ownership
Ownership not established by the available information.report unconfirmed ownership
land strip
Narrow area of ground.identify the land strip
beyond the fence
On the far side of the named fence.clarify beyond the fence
within the plan
Inside the assigned scope or area.stay within the plan
adjacent land
Land next to the current site or work area.distinguish adjacent land
access permission
Authorization to enter a specific area.verify access permission
entry request
Request to go into an area for work.refer an entry request
clearance request
Request to remove vegetation or debris from a specified area.define the clearance request
site plan
Document or drawing identifying site areas and planned work.consult the site plan
survey
Professional measurement or assessment of land boundaries or features.distinguish a survey from a visual guess
fence alignment
Position of the fence relative to the surrounding site.describe the fence alignment
assumption
Belief accepted without adequate verification.avoid an ownership assumption
qualified statement
Wording that preserves uncertainty or a stated limit.retain a qualified statement
scope extension
Proposed expansion of the assigned work area or tasks.request a scope extension
authorized area
Area for which relevant work permission is established.confirm the authorized area
boundary clarification
Process of resolving uncertainty about the relevant property limit.seek boundary clarification
office referral
Transfer of a question to the coordinating office.make an office referral
site restriction
Limit governing access or work at the location.respect the site restriction
revised plan
Updated work arrangement after the appropriate review and approval.await a revised plan

Access permission, property ownership, and the booked work area are separate questions. The dialogue supplies no legal determination or permission to enter and clear beyond the fence. Refer the request through the actual process before any proposed scope extension.

C Notice the language

A work limit is not a legal boundary finding

A fence can be a clear reference for the booked work without proving ownership of the land beside it. Repeat the customer request respectfully, preserve uncertain wording such as probably, and refer the issue instead of deciding property rights during a maintenance visit.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the requested area
You mean the strip beyond the rear fence?
Confirm the task
You would like that strip cleared.
State the current limit
Our work plan ends at the fence.
Preserve uncertainty
You said it is probably yours; ownership is not confirmed.
Separate plan and title
The work-plan limit does not determine the property line.
Avoid a legal conclusion
I cannot decide who owns that strip.
Avoid an access assumption
I cannot assume permission to enter beyond the fence.
Offer referral
I can flag the request to the office.
Keep the issue specific
The question concerns the strip beyond the rear fence.
Avoid a visual shortcut
The fence position alone does not settle ownership here.
Name the required review
The office needs to review the request and the access question.
Preserve the existing plan
No extension of the work area is approved.
Avoid claiming a survey
We have not established the boundary through this conversation.
Attribute the statement
The customer believes the strip may belong to the property.
Keep the outcome open
No revised plan is confirmed yet.
Close with the next step
I will pass on the clearance request with ownership and permission still unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Probably
Signals uncertainty and must not be rewritten as definite ownership.
Beyond versus within
Distinguishes the requested strip from the current planned area.
Ends at
Defines the work-plan limit without establishing a legal property line.
Believes
Attributes the claim rather than endorsing it as verified fact.
Permission versus ownership
These are related but separate questions requiring the appropriate process.
Referred versus approved
Passing a request to the office does not extend the work area.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report preserves the uncertainty?

Answer and explanation

B. Rosa believes the strip may be theirs; ownership is unconfirmed.

The correct report attributes Rosa's belief while retaining the lack of confirmation.

2. What does the rear fence establish in this scenario?

Answer and explanation

C. The endpoint of the current work plan.

The fence is the supplied work-plan reference, not a legal ownership determination.

3. Which action is within Kai's stated role?

Answer and explanation

D. Refer the request and access uncertainty to the office.

Kai can flag the issue but cannot decide ownership or extend the plan.

4. Which statement wrongly combines separate questions?

Answer and explanation

C. If the customer believes it is theirs, entry and clearance are automatically approved.

An uncertain ownership belief establishes neither access permission nor authorization for additional work.

The plan stops at the fence

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Customer / Kai: Grounds worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • scope extension
  • revised plan
  • land strip

  • fence alignment

  1. Rosa
    Could you clear the strip beyond the rear fence? I think it's probably ours, but I'm not certain.
  2. Kai
    You mean the 1 beyond the fence, not the ground on this side? I want to make sure I pass on the correct area.
  3. Rosa
    Yes, that narrow strip on the far side. The fence looks as though it's inside the edge, but I haven't confirmed it.
  4. Kai
    Thank you for clarifying. The current 2 is the rear fence. That tells me where this job ends, but it does not establish the legal boundary.
  5. Rosa
    I'd hoped you could include it while the crew was here. Does asking now change the work area?
  6. Kai
    It creates an 3 to refer, not an approved extension. I cannot decide the ownership question or assume permission to enter beyond the fence.
  7. Rosa
    Please keep probably in the note. I don't want it recorded as though I've established ownership.
  8. Kai
    I will keep the 4 accurate: you believe the strip may belong to the property, but ownership has not been confirmed.
  9. Rosa
    Does the fence position help? The planting continues past it, but that may not tell us much.
  10. Kai
    The 5 alone does not settle ownership here. A planting pattern is not enough for me to make a property-line determination either.
  11. Rosa
    All right. I'd rather check than ask you to clear land that turns out not to be ours.
  12. Kai
    I can make an 6. I will identify the strip, the clearance request, and the uncertainty about ownership and access.
  13. Rosa
    Please refer it. I'm asking about clearance, not saying you've agreed to do it today.
  14. Kai
    Correct. No 7 has been approved. The current plan still ends at the fence while the additional request is reviewed.
  15. Rosa
    If I find any ownership information, should I send it to the office for their review?
  16. Kai
    Yes. The relevant 8 and access review need the proper process. This conversation is not a survey or a legal decision about the strip.
  17. Rosa
    Could you read back the exact area? I don't mean everything behind the property.
  18. Kai
    You request clearance of the strip 9. Ownership is unconfirmed, and no permission or extension of the current work area has been established here.
  19. Rosa
    That's the request. Please keep the uncertainty in the referral, and I'll wait for the office.
  20. Kai
    I will pass it on. A 10 is not confirmed yet. The existing work limit remains the fence, with the extra request and its uncertainties clearly recorded.

Answers and explanations
  1. land strip Turn 2. Land strip identifies the narrow area beyond the rear fence, separate from the current planned work area.
  2. work-plan limit Turn 4. Work-plan limit is the rear fence, which defines assigned work without deciding the legal property boundary.
  3. additional request Turn 6. Additional request records Rosa's proposed extra work but does not itself extend the approved area.
  4. qualified statement Turn 8. Qualified statement preserves probably or may, rather than changing an uncertain ownership belief into a verified fact.
  5. fence alignment Turn 10. Fence alignment is an observed location feature and does not establish ownership or the property's legal limit in this case.
  6. office referral Turn 12. Office referral passes the precise clearance request and unresolved access question to the coordinating office for review.
  7. scope extension Turn 14. Scope extension would expand the work area beyond the fence, which has not been approved.
  8. boundary clarification Turn 16. Boundary clarification requires the appropriate review process, not an on-site ownership decision by the grounds worker.
  9. beyond the fence Turn 18. Beyond the fence precisely locates the additional area outside the current work-plan limit.
  10. revised plan Turn 20. Revised plan remains unconfirmed; referring the request has not approved entry or clearance beyond the fence.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: Could you clear the strip beyond the rear fence? I think it's probably ours, but I'm not certain.
  2. Kai: You mean the land strip beyond the fence, not the ground on this side? I want to make sure I pass on the correct area.
  3. Rosa: Yes, that narrow strip on the far side. The fence looks as though it's inside the edge, but I haven't confirmed it.
  4. Kai: Thank you for clarifying. The current work-plan limit is the rear fence. That tells me where this job ends, but it does not establish the legal boundary.
  5. Rosa: I'd hoped you could include it while the crew was here. Does asking now change the work area?
  6. Kai: It creates an additional request to refer, not an approved extension. I cannot decide the ownership question or assume permission to enter beyond the fence.
  7. Rosa: Please keep probably in the note. I don't want it recorded as though I've established ownership.
  8. Kai: I will keep the qualified statement accurate: you believe the strip may belong to the property, but ownership has not been confirmed.
  9. Rosa: Does the fence position help? The planting continues past it, but that may not tell us much.
  10. Kai: The fence alignment alone does not settle ownership here. A planting pattern is not enough for me to make a property-line determination either.
  11. Rosa: All right. I'd rather check than ask you to clear land that turns out not to be ours.
  12. Kai: I can make an office referral. I will identify the strip, the clearance request, and the uncertainty about ownership and access.
  13. Rosa: Please refer it. I'm asking about clearance, not saying you've agreed to do it today.
  14. Kai: Correct. No scope extension has been approved. The current plan still ends at the fence while the additional request is reviewed.
  15. Rosa: If I find any ownership information, should I send it to the office for their review?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Beyond the rear fence

A grounds worker responds to an uncertain property-area request.

  1. Customer: Could you clear beyond the fence? I think that strip is ours.
  2. Worker: The confirmed work plan ends at the fence. The extra strip isn't included in today's area.
  3. Customer: But the previous owner used it. Doesn't that make it reasonable to include while you're here?
  4. Worker: I can't establish the property boundary from that history. The office needs to review the request.
  5. Customer: Then you're not saying it belongs to someone else; you're saying it hasn't been confirmed?
  6. Worker: Exactly. I'm describing the limit of this job, not making a legal ownership decision.
  7. Customer: Can your team continue within the confirmed area while I contact the office?
  8. Worker: Yes, subject to the lead's site decisions. I'll keep the extra-area request separate in the handoff.
  9. Customer: Please call it the strip beyond the rear fence, not the whole back garden.
  10. Worker: I'll identify the strip without changing today's confirmed work boundary.
A locked gate is an access issue

A worker calls the named site contact about a closed service entrance.

  1. Worker: The agreed courtyard service gate is locked; we've arrived for the visit.
  2. Site contact: I thought reception had arranged access. Have you spoken with anyone who can confirm today's entry arrangement?
  3. Worker: Reception said they weren't given the booking. I haven't treated another entrance as approved access.
  4. Site contact: I can coordinate with the building team. Please keep the issue described as access unconfirmed, not customer cancellation.
  5. Worker: Understood. The scheduled work still exists; we can't yet reach the agreed service area.
  6. Site contact: The building contact will call you in ten minutes with a confirmed arrangement or a status update.
  7. Worker: Ten minutes means an update, not guaranteed access, correct?
  8. Site contact: Yes. I don't want you planning around an opening time the building team hasn't confirmed.
  9. Worker: I'll tell the lead the access check is underway and keep the rest of the visit status accurate.
  10. Site contact: I'll give reception the courtyard work-order reference when checking access.
A marked no-work area

A worker confirms an excluded flower bed with a facilities contact.

  1. Worker: The bed beside the west bench is marked as a no-work area today.
  2. Facilities contact: That's correct. Another team is reviewing it, and this grounds visit should exclude that particular bed.
  3. Worker: A resident asked us to include it because the rest of the courtyard is being serviced.
  4. Facilities contact: Please explain that the bed has a specific exclusion, not that the entire courtyard visit is canceled.
  5. Worker: Should I refer their question to you rather than try to explain the other team's technical work?
  6. Facilities contact: Yes. You can identify me as the site contact for that request without guessing what their review involves.
  7. Worker: West-bench bed, not the east entrance bed; is that correct?
  8. Facilities contact: Exactly. Those two locations are easy to confuse when people just say the bench area.
  9. Worker: The crew's handoff will preserve that named exclusion while showing the other listed areas unchanged.
  10. Facilities contact: I'll explain the separate review requirement to the resident.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The plan stops at the fence
  1. Read the fence as the work-plan limit, not a verified property line.
  2. Repeat Rosa's probably or may wording when attributing her ownership belief.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Refer another uncertain area request

A work plan ends at a side gate. A customer asks for clearance beyond it and says the area may belong to the property. Ownership and access are unconfirmed; the worker can refer the request to the office.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The current plan ends at the side ___."

Answer and explanation

B. gate

The side gate is the stated endpoint of the existing work plan.

2. Customer: "The area ___ belong to the property."

Answer and explanation

D. may

May preserves the customer's uncertain ownership statement rather than asserting a fact.

3. Worker: "Ownership remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unconfirmed

No evidence in the scenario establishes who owns the requested area.

4. Customer: "The next step is office ___."

Answer and explanation

B. review

The request can be reviewed, but entry and clearance are not yet authorized.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

This book teaches workplace English, not horticultural diagnosis, pruning technique, equipment operation, chemical application, land surveying, or legal boundary advice. Follow actual site controls, training, work orders, local requirements, and qualified-lead decisions. The dialogues do not authorize entering disputed land, pruning a plant, changing irrigation, applying products, substituting materials, or resuming paused work.
Lesson 02: Clarifying access and property limits
Original fictional case: At Elm View, customer Rosa asks grounds worker Kai to clear a strip beyond the rear fence. Rosa says the strip is probably part of the property, but ownership is unconfirmed. The current work plan ends at the fence. Kai can report the additional request and uncertainty to the office but cannot determine the property boundary, extend the work area, or assume permission to enter. No ownership conclusion, clearance work beyond the fence, or revised plan is established.
Language workshop: The plan stops at the fence
Communication goal: Separate a work-plan limit from an unverified ownership claim and refer an access request without deciding a legal boundary.
Language guidance: The work-plan limit is the rear fence. That fact does not prove the legal property line or ownership of the strip beyond it. Rosa says probably; the uncertainty must remain explicit in the office referral.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You mean the strip beyond the rear fence? / You would like that strip cleared. / Our work plan ends at the fence. / You said it is probably yours; ownership is not confirmed. / The work-plan limit does not determine the property line. / I cannot decide who owns that strip. / I cannot assume permission to enter beyond the fence. / I can flag the request to the office. / The question concerns the strip beyond the rear fence. / The fence position alone does not settle ownership here. / The office needs to review the request and the access question. / No extension of the work area is approved. / We have not established the boundary through this conversation. / The customer believes the strip may belong to the property. / No revised plan is confirmed yet. / I will pass on the clearance request with ownership and permission still unresolved.
Vocabulary: work-plan limit: Endpoint of the area covered by the current assigned work. / rear fence: Fence at the back of the site used as a location reference. / boundary: Line separating areas or property interests, requiring appropriate verification. / property line: Legally relevant limit of a property, not established here. / ownership claim: Statement that land belongs to someone. / unconfirmed ownership: Ownership not established by the available information. / land strip: Narrow area of ground. / beyond the fence: On the far side of the named fence. / within the plan: Inside the assigned scope or area. / adjacent land: Land next to the current site or work area. / access permission: Authorization to enter a specific area. / entry request: Request to go into an area for work. / clearance request: Request to remove vegetation or debris from a specified area. / site plan: Document or drawing identifying site areas and planned work. / survey: Professional measurement or assessment of land boundaries or features. / fence alignment: Position of the fence relative to the surrounding site. / assumption: Belief accepted without adequate verification. / qualified statement: Wording that preserves uncertainty or a stated limit. / scope extension: Proposed expansion of the assigned work area or tasks. / authorized area: Area for which relevant work permission is established. / boundary clarification: Process of resolving uncertainty about the relevant property limit. / office referral: Transfer of a question to the coordinating office. / site restriction: Limit governing access or work at the location. / revised plan: Updated work arrangement after the appropriate review and approval.
Speaking task: Read the fence as the work-plan limit, not a verified property line. Repeat Rosa's probably or may wording when attributing her ownership belief. Read the office referral without treating it as permission to enter the strip.
Partner: Rosa: Customer / Kai: Grounds worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A work plan ends at a side gate. A customer asks for clearance beyond it and says the area may belong to the property. Ownership and access are unconfirmed; the worker can refer the request to the office.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Could you read back the exact area? I don't mean everything behind the property.
Kai: You request clearance of the strip beyond the fence. Ownership is unconfirmed, and no permission or extension of the current work area has been established here.
Rosa: That's the request. Please keep the uncertainty in the referral, and I'll wait for the office.
Kai: I will pass it on. A revised plan is not confirmed yet. The existing work limit remains the fence, with the extra request and its uncertainties clearly recorded.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
