03Clarifying appearance and plant requestsWhich plant needs attention?+

Your goal

Resolve an ambiguous plant reference, describe the requested appearance, and preserve the qualified lead review.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Which plant needs attention?

At the entrance to Maple House, customer Nina asks worker Tomas to tidy the tall one. Two plants stand nearby: rosemary beside the left flower bed and a specimen magnolia. Nina means the rosemary, not the magnolia. She wants a neater appearance but has not specified a suitable pruning method or extent. A qualified lead must assess whether and how any pruning fits the job. Identifying the plant does not authorize the worker to cut it.

Nina: Customer / Tomas: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which plant does Nina mean?

Answer and explanation

B. The rosemary beside the left flower bed

Nina clarifies that the rosemary, not the magnolia, is the requested plant.

2. What result does she request?

Answer and explanation

A. A neater appearance

Nina describes an appearance goal without specifying a suitable method or extent.

3. What remains necessary?

Answer and explanation

D. Qualified lead review of whether and how pruning fits the job

Plant identification resolves the reference but does not establish authorization or appropriate pruning.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The rosemary is identified, while the magnolia is excluded from this request. Nina wants a neater appearance. Neither that preference nor the clearer plant reference determines a suitable pruning method, pruning extent, or permission to start cutting.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
plant identification
Establishing which plant a person is referring to.confirm plant identification
rosemary
Named plant in the customer's request.identify the rosemary
specimen plant
Individual plant grown as a distinct feature.identify the specimen plant
magnolia
Named plant excluded from this particular request.distinguish the magnolia
flower bed
Defined area containing ornamental plants.locate the left flower bed
entrance planting
Plants positioned near the property entrance.describe the entrance planting
appearance goal
Visual result the customer wants.clarify the appearance goal
tidy up
Informal request to make something look neater.clarify what tidy up means
pruning
Selective removal of plant parts for a defined purpose.refer a pruning request
plant form
Overall shape and structure of a plant.describe the plant form
growth habit
Characteristic way a plant grows.assess the growth habit
canopy
Above-ground spread of a woody plant's branches and foliage.describe the canopy
foliage
Leaves collectively.refer to the foliage
branch
Woody growth extending from a stem or trunk.identify the branch
stem
Supporting axis bearing a plant's leaves or growth.identify the stem
plant health
Condition of the living plant.assess plant health
qualified assessment
Review by someone with the relevant competence.request a qualified assessment
pruning extent
Amount and reach of proposed pruning.clarify the pruning extent
proposed method
Suggested way to carry out a task.review the proposed method
reference point
Fixed feature used to clarify a location.use a reference point
cultivar
Named cultivated selection within a plant group, such as lavender Hidcote.confirm the cultivar name
customer preference
Result favored by the customer.record the customer preference
botanical name
Scientific plant name identifying genus and species, with cultivar where relevant.read the full botanical name
cutting authorization
Permission to perform specified cutting work.confirm cutting authorization

Tidy up is useful as the beginning of a conversation, but it is not a precise work instruction. The lead must assess the request and its fit with the job. Avoid converting an informal appearance goal into removal, treatment, or immediate pruning.

C Notice the language

A description is not a technical instruction

Customers often describe the visual result they want rather than a horticultural method. Repeat the plant and location, clarify the appearance goal, and pass the request to the qualified lead without turning casual words into an agreed cutting instruction.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the reference
Which plant do you mean by the tall one?
Offer the two candidates
Do you mean the rosemary or the specimen magnolia?
Use the location
Is it the rosemary beside the left flower bed?
Confirm the exclusion
You are not asking us to work on the magnolia.
Clarify the result
What would you like to look neater?
Preserve the wording
I will record a neater appearance as your goal.
Avoid an invented diagnosis
I have not assessed the plant's health.
Separate identification and permission
We have identified the plant, but pruning is not yet authorized.
Refer the method
The qualified lead needs to assess how to approach it.
Refer the extent
The amount of pruning is still to be assessed.
Check the job fit
The lead also needs to check whether it fits this job.
Avoid promising removal
I am not recording a removal request.
Read back the request
Rosemary beside the left flower bed, with a neater appearance requested.
Keep the other plant separate
The magnolia is outside this request.
Explain the next step
I will pass the specific request to the lead.
Close without approval
No cutting decision has been made in this conversation.
6 language and precision notes
The tall one
An ambiguous reference when more than one plant could fit the description.
Beside
Gives a location relative to a visible reference point.
Not the magnolia
Explicitly excludes the other candidate from the request.
Neater
Describes an appearance preference without specifying a method.
Whether and how
Preserves both job suitability and the appropriate approach for review.
Identified versus authorized
Knowing the subject does not establish permission to perform the task.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which clarification resolves the ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

A. Do you mean the rosemary beside the left flower bed, not the magnolia?

The question identifies the intended plant and explicitly distinguishes the nearby alternative.

2. Which record preserves Nina's request?

Answer and explanation

C. Rosemary beside the left flower bed; neater appearance requested; lead review needed.

The record preserves the plant, appearance goal, and unresolved qualified assessment.

3. Which statement goes beyond the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. The rosemary definitely needs a specific amount cut off today.

Neither a suitable amount nor same-day cutting permission has been established.

4. What can Tomas confirm now?

Answer and explanation

C. The plant and the requested visual goal

Tomas can clarify the reference and preference while leaving technical and scope decisions open.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Which plant needs attention?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nina: Customer / Tomas: Grounds worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • appearance goal
  • Scope fit
  • proposed method
  • plant identification
  • reference point
  • Cutting authorization
  • customer preference
  • qualified assessment
  • left-hand bed
  • pruning extent
  1. Nina
    Could you tidy the tall one by the entrance? I mean the one beside that bed; it looks a bit untidy.
  2. Tomas
    For 1, do you mean the rosemary beside the left flower bed, or the specimen magnolia? Both are near the entrance.
  3. Nina
    The rosemary, please. Not the magnolia. I can see why the tall one wasn't a very useful description with both here.
  4. Tomas
    I'll use the left bed as the 2 and name the rosemary in the note. That should keep the two plants separate.
  5. Nina
    I don't know the gardening term for what I want. Neater is probably the best way I can describe it.
  6. Tomas
    I can record that 3 without choosing a cutting method. The lead needs to assess what is suitable for this plant and this job.
  7. Nina
    That's fine. I don't want someone to take off a large amount just because I said tidy it up.
  8. Tomas
    The 4 hasn't been decided. We've identified the plant, but not how much, if anything, should be removed.
  9. Nina
    And please don't put removal in the note. I want to keep the rosemary; I'm asking about how it looks.
  10. Tomas
    I'll preserve that 5. Neater appearance, not removal, and no request to work on the magnolia.
  11. Nina
    Does the lead also need to check whether this is part of our booking? I wasn't sure if I could add it while you're here.
  12. Tomas
    Yes, 6 still needs review. An appearance request doesn't automatically add pruning to the work order.
  13. Nina
    Could the lead explain what they're proposing before anything is cut? I'd like to know what the result would mean.
  14. Tomas
    The 7 needs that assessment first. I'm not recommending a technique or promising a particular result from this conversation.
  15. Nina
    Please read the location back for me. The entrance has several plants, and I don't want the wrong one included.
  16. Tomas
    Rosemary beside the 8 at the entrance; neater appearance requested; magnolia excluded. The qualified lead reviews suitability and whether it belongs to this job.
  17. Nina
    Yes, that's the one. I'm agreeing that you've understood my request, not agreeing to an amount of cutting today.
  18. Tomas
    9 isn't established by that clarification. I'll keep the request separate from any later decision to proceed.
  19. Nina
    Thank you. Send that to the lead, please. I'll wait to hear the assessment rather than choose a pruning amount myself.
  20. Tomas
    I'll refer it for a 10, with the plant and your appearance goal clear. No pruning has been agreed or reported complete.

Answers and explanations
  1. plant identification Turn 2. Plant identification establishes the intended subject before the worker records or refers any pruning request.
  2. reference point Turn 4. Reference point uses the left flower bed to distinguish the rosemary from the nearby magnolia.
  3. appearance goal Turn 6. Appearance goal records the visual result Nina wants without inventing a pruning method or amount.
  4. pruning extent Turn 8. Pruning extent concerns how much work would be appropriate; Nina has not specified or authorized that amount.
  5. customer preference Turn 10. Customer preference is for a neater appearance, not plant removal or a particular technical treatment.
  6. Scope fit Turn 12. Scope fit asks whether the requested pruning belongs to the agreed job; the conversation has not settled that question.
  7. proposed method Turn 14. Proposed method remains a matter for qualified assessment rather than a cutting instruction supplied by the customer.
  8. left-hand bed Turn 16. Left-hand bed preserves Nina's specific location and helps the lead identify the correct plant at the entrance.
  9. Cutting authorization Turn 18. Cutting authorization has not been granted merely by clarifying the plant and describing a preferred appearance.
  10. qualified assessment Turn 20. Qualified assessment is the next step for the request; no pruning result, method, or completion is promised.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nina: Could you tidy the tall one by the entrance? I mean the one beside that bed; it looks a bit untidy.
  2. Tomas: For plant identification, do you mean the rosemary beside the left flower bed, or the specimen magnolia? Both are near the entrance.
  3. Nina: The rosemary, please. Not the magnolia. I can see why the tall one wasn't a very useful description with both here.
  4. Tomas: I'll use the left bed as the reference point and name the rosemary in the note. That should keep the two plants separate.
  5. Nina: I don't know the gardening term for what I want. Neater is probably the best way I can describe it.
  6. Tomas: I can record that appearance goal without choosing a cutting method. The lead needs to assess what is suitable for this plant and this job.
  7. Nina: That's fine. I don't want someone to take off a large amount just because I said tidy it up.
  8. Tomas: The pruning extent hasn't been decided. We've identified the plant, but not how much, if anything, should be removed.
  9. Nina: And please don't put removal in the note. I want to keep the rosemary; I'm asking about how it looks.
  10. Tomas: I'll preserve that customer preference. Neater appearance, not removal, and no request to work on the magnolia.
  11. Nina: Does the lead also need to check whether this is part of our booking? I wasn't sure if I could add it while you're here.
  12. Tomas: Yes, Scope fit still needs review. An appearance request doesn't automatically add pruning to the work order.
  13. Nina: Could the lead explain what they're proposing before anything is cut? I'd like to know what the result would mean.
  14. Tomas: The proposed method needs that assessment first. I'm not recommending a technique or promising a particular result from this conversation.
  15. Nina: Please read the location back for me. The entrance has several plants, and I don't want the wrong one included.
  16. Tomas: Rosemary beside the left-hand bed at the entrance; neater appearance requested; magnolia excluded. The qualified lead reviews suitability and whether it belongs to this job.
  17. Nina: Yes, that's the one. I'm agreeing that you've understood my request, not agreeing to an amount of cutting today.
  18. Tomas: Cutting authorization isn't established by that clarification. I'll keep the request separate from any later decision to proceed.
  19. Nina: Thank you. Send that to the lead, please. I'll wait to hear the assessment rather than choose a pruning amount myself.
  20. Tomas: I'll refer it for a qualified assessment, with the plant and your appearance goal clear. No pruning has been agreed or reported complete.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which tall plant?

A worker clarifies an appearance request beside the entrance.

  1. Customer: Could someone tidy the tall plant? Its outline looks uneven.
  2. Worker: Do you mean the rosemary beside the left flower bed or the magnolia near the door?
  3. Customer: The rosemary. Please leave the magnolia out of this request; it's our specimen plant.
  4. Worker: Thanks. What part of the appearance concerns you so I can relay the request accurately?
  5. Customer: It looks uneven from the path. Could your lead take a look?
  6. Worker: I'll relay that pruning request to the lead for a suitability and scope review.
  7. Customer: Would reshaping it guarantee more flowers as well?
  8. Worker: I can't promise that outcome. The lead can discuss the plant question with you separately.
  9. Customer: Yes, the rosemary beside the left bed. Please leave the magnolia alone.
  10. Worker: I'll relay that specific appearance request without treating it as a pruning instruction.
A photo shows a preference, not a method

A grounds worker discusses a reference image with a customer.

  1. Customer: Could our entrance hedge resemble the outline in this photograph?
  2. Worker: The photograph helps show your preference, but I can't promise the same result for a different hedge.
  3. Customer: I mainly like the even outline. I don't know whether the plants are the same type.
  4. Worker: Then I'll describe the desired outline and leave plant suitability and any method to the lead.
  5. Customer: Please don't tell the lead I requested removing half of it. That's not what I meant.
  6. Worker: I won't convert a visual example into an invented amount of work or a cutting instruction.
  7. Customer: Please preserve the entrance view; don't include the side hedge.
  8. Worker: Yes. I'll identify the entrance hedge as the subject and the side hedge as outside this request.
  9. Customer: That gives the lead enough context to discuss options with me rather than copy the photo blindly.
  10. Worker: The photograph expresses a preference; actual work still needs review.
The bed name changed in the customer's description

A worker and customer establish which planting area a request concerns.

  1. Worker: Your message says kitchen bed; our plan uses compass directions.
  2. Customer: I mean the narrow bed outside the kitchen window, which faces the courtyard on the south side.
  3. Worker: Then is that the south courtyard bed, rather than the large bed beside the south gate?
  4. Customer: Yes. Both face south, but only the narrow one is directly under the kitchen window.
  5. Worker: Could you describe the appearance concern without naming a treatment you aren't certain about?
  6. Customer: The plants look uneven in height. I'd like the lead to discuss a more consistent appearance.
  7. Worker: I'll specify the narrow bed under the kitchen window, not the gate bed.
  8. Customer: Please also say the container by the door is separate and should stay out of the request.
  9. Worker: I have both limits. No plant method or replacement choice has been agreed during this clarification.
  10. Customer: Correct. We're clarifying the area and appearance, not choosing a method.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Which plant needs attention?
  1. Read rosemary beside the left flower bed, then explicitly exclude the magnolia.
  2. Repeat neater appearance without replacing it with a pruning amount or removal.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back the plant request

Complete this exchange using the clarified plant, the excluded plant, the appearance goal, and the unresolved review. Do not add a pruning amount or approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "You mean the ___ beside the left flower bed."

Answer and explanation

A. rosemary

Rosemary is the plant Nina explicitly identifies beside the left bed.

2. Customer: "The ___ is not part of my request."

Answer and explanation

D. magnolia

Nina excludes the nearby magnolia from the appearance request.

3. Worker: "Your goal is a ___ appearance."

Answer and explanation

B. neater

Neater preserves the customer preference without prescribing a pruning method.

4. Customer: "The qualified lead still needs to ___ it."

Answer and explanation

D. assess

Assessment remains necessary before any method, extent, or authorization is established.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Original fictional case: At the entrance to Maple House, customer Nina asks worker Tomas to tidy the tall one. Two plants stand nearby: rosemary beside the left flower bed and a specimen magnolia. Nina means the rosemary, not the magnolia. She wants a neater appearance but has not specified a suitable pruning method or extent. A qualified lead must assess whether and how any pruning fits the job. Identifying the plant does not authorize the worker to cut it.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Explaining a weather-related changeA suggested day is not a booking+

Your goal

Explain a work pause, preserve the customer deadline, and distinguish a requested update from a confirmed return visit.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A suggested day is not a booking

At Cedar Court, lead Imani has paused planned outdoor work because of site conditions after rain. Customer Joel has an event on Friday. The office has suggested Thursday as a possible return day, but crew availability is unconfirmed. Imani can relay the event and request a scheduling update by Wednesday noon. Neither the Thursday visit nor completion before the event is promised. The customer needs an accurate status and a clear next communication point.

Joel: Customer / Imani: Grounds lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why is the work paused?

Answer and explanation

D. Site conditions after rain

The lead has paused the planned work because of the site conditions after rain.

2. What is the status of Thursday?

Answer and explanation

B. Suggested, with crew availability unconfirmed

Thursday is only an office suggestion because crew availability has not been confirmed.

3. What communication can Imani request?

Answer and explanation

C. A scheduling update by Wednesday noon

Imani can request the update while preserving the unresolved visit and completion dates.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Thursday is a suggested return day, not a confirmed booking. Wednesday noon is the requested scheduling-update time, not a work-completion deadline. Friday is the customer event. Keep these three time references separate when summarizing the conversation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
weather pause
Temporary stop associated with weather-related conditions.explain a weather pause
site conditions
Physical circumstances at the work location.assess site conditions
rainfall
Rain received at a location.refer to recent rainfall
ground condition
State of the surface or soil at the site.review the ground condition
waterlogged
Saturated with water beyond normal drainage.describe a waterlogged area
planned work
Tasks intended for the scheduled visit.identify the planned work
resumption
Starting work again after a pause.confirm authorization for resumption
tentative date
Proposed day that is not yet confirmed.state a tentative date
crew availability
Whether the required workers are available.confirm crew availability
scheduling update
New information about arrangements for work.request a scheduling update
return visit
Later attendance to continue or review work.discuss a return visit
event deadline
Time constraint created by a planned event.relay the event deadline
completion date
Day on which work is expected or agreed to finish.avoid an unsupported completion date
weather dependency
Reliance on suitable weather-related circumstances.explain a weather dependency
provisional suggestion
Idea offered before arrangements are confirmed.label a provisional suggestion
customer constraint
Limitation affecting what the customer needs.record a customer constraint
office coordinator
Person arranging visits and communication.contact the office coordinator
confirmation status
Whether an arrangement is agreed or remains open.state the confirmation status
update target
Requested time for the next communication.specify the update target
service interruption
Break in planned service delivery.explain a service interruption
revised schedule
Changed arrangement for when work occurs.confirm a revised schedule
outstanding check
Matter still requiring verification.name the outstanding check
deadline pressure
Urgency arising from a time limit.acknowledge deadline pressure
work status
Current stage of the assigned work.report the work status

The lead has paused work because of site conditions after rain. The book does not prescribe when those conditions become suitable. Neither customer urgency nor a calendar suggestion authorizes resumption or establishes that the work will be complete before the event.

C Notice the language

Give useful certainty at the right level

When a customer faces a deadline, a possible day can easily sound like a promise. State the known pause, the suggested day, the unresolved availability, and the requested update separately. Acknowledge the event without guaranteeing work that is not scheduled.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the pause
The planned outdoor work is paused because of the site conditions after rain.
Acknowledge the impact
I understand the Friday event makes the timing important.
State the suggestion
The office has suggested Thursday.
Preserve uncertainty
Crew availability has not been confirmed.
Correct an assumption
Thursday is a possibility, not a booked return visit.
Separate the deadline
Friday is your event date, not a completion promise from us.
Relay the constraint
I will pass the Friday event detail to the office.
Request the update
I will request a scheduling update by Wednesday noon.
Distinguish the two times
Wednesday noon concerns the update, not the work itself.
Avoid a guarantee
I cannot guarantee completion before the event.
Keep the pause intact
The suggestion does not authorize work to resume now.
Name the unresolved check
Availability still needs to be checked.
Avoid inventing a cause
I am reporting the site conditions, not diagnosing a drainage fault.
Repeat the current status
Work is paused and no return date is confirmed.
Preserve customer planning
Please do not treat Thursday as a confirmed arrangement yet.
Close with the handoff
I will relay your event deadline and the requested update time.
6 language and precision notes
Paused
Describes the present work status without claiming permanent cancellation.
Suggested
Marks a proposed date rather than an agreed appointment.
By Wednesday noon
Gives the requested communication deadline, not a service deadline.
Before Friday
Refers to the desired timing relative to the customer event.
Unconfirmed
Leaves availability open without implying that it is definitely unavailable.
Request versus promise
Asking for an update does not prove the office has accepted that deadline.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation preserves all three dates?

Answer and explanation

D. Thursday is suggested; an update is requested by Wednesday noon; the event is Friday.

The explanation assigns each date its actual role without converting suggestions into promises.

2. Which statement should be corrected?

Answer and explanation

A. Thursday is definitely booked and everything will be finished before Friday.

Neither the Thursday booking nor completion before the event is confirmed.

3. What should Imani send the office?

Answer and explanation

C. The Friday event detail and a request for a scheduling update by Wednesday noon

These are the customer constraint and communication request actually supported by the case.

4. What does unconfirmed availability mean?

Answer and explanation

B. The crew arrangement still needs verification

Unconfirmed preserves the open check rather than asserting availability or unavailability.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A suggested day is not a booking

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Joel: Customer / Imani: Grounds lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • tentative date
  • event deadline
  • work status
  • outstanding check
  • office coordinator
  • customer constraint
  • confirmation status
  • resumption
  • scheduling update
  • service interruption
  1. Joel
    I heard Thursday mentioned. We've got an event on Friday; can I tell the organizer the work will be finished?
  2. Imani
    Not yet. The current 1 is paused because of the site conditions after rain. I understand why you need a reliable answer before your event.
  3. Joel
    Thursday sounded definite when I heard it. Is a crew assigned, or was it only a possible day?
  4. Imani
    It is a 2. The office suggested Thursday, but crew availability has not been confirmed. Please do not treat that suggestion as a booked visit.
  5. Joel
    I'm not asking you to ignore the site conditions. I just need to know what arrangements I can rely on.
  6. Imani
    I will relay that 3 clearly. Friday is your event date; it is not a completion commitment that we have made, and I should keep those separate.
  7. Joel
    Can we ask for an update before Wednesday lunchtime? Friday morning would be too late for my planning.
  8. Imani
    I can request a 4 by Wednesday noon. That is a request for the next communication, not a promise that the work will happen then.
  9. Joel
    So Wednesday noon is the update request, and Thursday is still only the possible return day.
  10. Imani
    Exactly. The 5 of Thursday remains open. The office still needs to check availability before a return arrangement can be confirmed.
  11. Joel
    Please include the Friday event in the message. That's why I need the scheduling answer.
  12. Imani
    Yes. I will include your 6 and the requested Wednesday-noon update together. The message will say the timing matters for your event, without guaranteeing a result.
  13. Joel
    Has the pause actually been lifted, or is Thursday just a calendar suggestion at this stage?
  14. Imani
    The suggestion does not establish 7. The pause remains the current status, and I will not present a calendar suggestion as permission to restart the work.
  15. Joel
    I'd prefer an honest unresolved update to a date that sounds certain and then falls through.
  16. Imani
    I will make the 8 explicit. Crew availability is unconfirmed, and no return date or completion date has been agreed in this conversation.
  17. Joel
    Please request Wednesday noon and flag Friday. I won't tell the organizer Thursday is booked.
  18. Imani
    I will pass both details to the 9. Requesting the update does not mean the office has already accepted the deadline, so I will describe it accurately.
  19. Joel
    Thanks. Work paused, Thursday suggested, update requested by Wednesday noon, event Friday. I'll keep those separate.
  20. Imani
    That is the correct summary of the 10. I will relay it without adding a booking or a promise that everything will be finished before your event.

Answers and explanations
  1. work status Turn 2. Work status is paused because of site conditions after rain; the conversation does not establish completion or resumption.
  2. tentative date Turn 4. Tentative date describes Thursday as a suggestion rather than a confirmed return visit or guaranteed completion date.
  3. customer constraint Turn 6. Customer constraint is the Friday event, which affects planning but does not authorize resuming work or promise completion.
  4. scheduling update Turn 8. Scheduling update is the information Imani can request by Wednesday noon; it is not the work itself.
  5. confirmation status Turn 10. Confirmation status keeps Thursday open because the office has not established crew availability or a confirmed return arrangement.
  6. event deadline Turn 12. Event deadline records Friday as the customer's planning constraint without treating it as an agreed service deadline.
  7. resumption Turn 14. Resumption means restarting paused work; a proposed date alone does not authorize it or establish suitable conditions.
  8. outstanding check Turn 16. Outstanding check is crew availability, which remains unresolved and must not be silently treated as confirmed.
  9. office coordinator Turn 18. Office coordinator is the scheduling contact who receives the event constraint and the requested communication time.
  10. service interruption Turn 20. Service interruption refers to the paused planned work, with a suggested return day and an unresolved scheduling arrangement.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Joel: I heard Thursday mentioned. We've got an event on Friday; can I tell the organizer the work will be finished?
  2. Imani: Not yet. The current work status is paused because of the site conditions after rain. I understand why you need a reliable answer before your event.
  3. Joel: Thursday sounded definite when I heard it. Is a crew assigned, or was it only a possible day?
  4. Imani: It is a tentative date. The office suggested Thursday, but crew availability has not been confirmed. Please do not treat that suggestion as a booked visit.
  5. Joel: I'm not asking you to ignore the site conditions. I just need to know what arrangements I can rely on.
  6. Imani: I will relay that customer constraint clearly. Friday is your event date; it is not a completion commitment that we have made, and I should keep those separate.
  7. Joel: Can we ask for an update before Wednesday lunchtime? Friday morning would be too late for my planning.
  8. Imani: I can request a scheduling update by Wednesday noon. That is a request for the next communication, not a promise that the work will happen then.
  9. Joel: So Wednesday noon is the update request, and Thursday is still only the possible return day.
  10. Imani: Exactly. The confirmation status of Thursday remains open. The office still needs to check availability before a return arrangement can be confirmed.
  11. Joel: Please include the Friday event in the message. That's why I need the scheduling answer.
  12. Imani: Yes. I will include your event deadline and the requested Wednesday-noon update together. The message will say the timing matters for your event, without guaranteeing a result.
  13. Joel: Has the pause actually been lifted, or is Thursday just a calendar suggestion at this stage?
  14. Imani: The suggestion does not establish resumption. The pause remains the current status, and I will not present a calendar suggestion as permission to restart the work.
  15. Joel: I'd prefer an honest unresolved update to a date that sounds certain and then falls through.
  16. Imani: I will make the outstanding check explicit. Crew availability is unconfirmed, and no return date or completion date has been agreed in this conversation.
  17. Joel: Please request Wednesday noon and flag Friday. I won't tell the organizer Thursday is booked.
  18. Imani: I will pass both details to the office coordinator. Requesting the update does not mean the office has already accepted the deadline, so I will describe it accurately.
  19. Joel: Thanks. Work paused, Thursday suggested, update requested by Wednesday noon, event Friday. I'll keep those separate.
  20. Imani: That is the correct summary of the service interruption. I will relay it without adding a booking or a promise that everything will be finished before your event.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Thursday remains provisional

A worker updates Cedar Court's customer about a paused visit.

  1. Worker: The lead paused today's outdoor work because of post-rain site conditions.
  2. Customer: The office mentioned Thursday. My event is Friday, so can I count on everything being finished beforehand?
  3. Worker: Thursday is a provisional date; crew availability and completion before your event remain unconfirmed.
  4. Customer: Please tell the office the front entrance matters most to us for Friday's visitors.
  5. Worker: I'll relay that priority as your request, not as a change already approved by the crew.
  6. Customer: Could someone give me an update by Wednesday noon even if the date remains uncertain?
  7. Worker: I'll request Wednesday noon's update, explaining your Friday event constraint.
  8. Customer: Then Wednesday noon is the update request, and Thursday is still only a suggested visit?
  9. Worker: Exactly. The office needs to confirm an actual replacement arrangement before we treat it as booked.
  10. Customer: Thanks. I'll keep Thursday provisional when discussing arrangements with our event team.
A visit moved, not removed

A worker clarifies a weather message with a customer.

  1. Customer: Does postponed mean this month's service has been canceled?
  2. Worker: No. It means the scheduled visit has been delayed; the replacement date is still being arranged.
  3. Customer: I was concerned we'd skip straight to next month and lose the work that was due.
  4. Worker: I can pass that concern to scheduling and ask how the delay affects the service interval.
  5. Customer: Please don't promise an extra visit unless the office has actually agreed one for our property.
  6. Worker: I won't. I'll ask for the revised arrangement rather than infer it from the word postponed.
  7. Customer: Is Tuesday confirmed, or merely a provisional replacement date?
  8. Worker: The note labels Tuesday provisional. The office still needs to confirm the crew and timing.
  9. Customer: Then please have them state the new date clearly when it is settled, not just send 'rescheduled.'
  10. Worker: I'll request a clear replacement date once scheduling actually confirms it.
Only one part of the visit changed

A worker explains a limited change to a facilities contact.

  1. Worker: The rear-lawn task is deferred; today's entrance-area review remains planned.
  2. Facilities contact: I heard weather delay and assumed the entire visit was canceled, including that review.
  3. Worker: The message was too broad. The change applies to the rear-lawn task, not every listed activity.
  4. Facilities contact: Thank you for correcting it. Has the lead confirmed the remaining plan under today's site conditions?
  5. Worker: Yes, that remaining review is confirmed by the lead. I am relaying the decision, not assessing conditions myself.
  6. Facilities contact: Then the completion note should show which task was deferred and which review actually took place.
  7. Worker: The rear-lawn follow-up still needs scheduling; no replacement date is confirmed.
  8. Facilities contact: Please send that question to the office rather than describing our next regular visit as the replacement automatically.
  9. Worker: I'll do that. The postponed task and the regular schedule will remain separate until scheduling confirms the arrangement.
  10. Facilities contact: That preserves today's remaining review and the separate deferred lawn task.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A suggested day is not a booking
  1. Read the three time references: Wednesday-noon update, suggested Thursday, Friday event.
  2. Repeat requested and suggested with emphasis; neither means confirmed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate three time references

Complete the customer update. Keep the pause, the suggested Thursday visit, the requested Wednesday-noon update, and the Friday event in their correct roles.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "The work remains ___ because of the site conditions."

Answer and explanation

A. paused

Paused is the stated work status after rain affected the site.

2. Customer: "Thursday is ___, not confirmed."

Answer and explanation

C. suggested

Thursday was proposed by the office, but crew availability remains unconfirmed.

3. Lead: "I will request an update by Wednesday ___."

Answer and explanation

C. noon

Noon completes the requested Wednesday communication time, not a work deadline.

4. Customer: "My event is on ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Friday

Friday is the event date and does not establish a completion guarantee.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Landscaping and Grounds English
Professional audience: Landscape crew members, groundskeepers, lawn-service workers, and grounds assistants
Scope: All sites, customers, plants, materials, times, work orders, and decisions in the cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not horticultural diagnosis, pruning technique, equipment operation, chemical application, land surveying, or legal boundary advice. Follow actual site controls, training, work orders, local requirements, and qualified-lead decisions. The dialogues do not authorize entering disputed land, pruning a plant, changing irrigation, applying products, substituting materials, or resuming paused work.
Lesson 04: Explaining a weather-related change
Original fictional case: At Cedar Court, lead Imani has paused planned outdoor work because of site conditions after rain. Customer Joel has an event on Friday. The office has suggested Thursday as a possible return day, but crew availability is unconfirmed. Imani can relay the event and request a scheduling update by Wednesday noon. Neither the Thursday visit nor completion before the event is promised. The customer needs an accurate status and a clear next communication point.
Language workshop: A suggested day is not a booking
Communication goal: Explain a work pause, preserve the customer deadline, and distinguish a requested update from a confirmed return visit.
Language guidance: Thursday is a suggested return day, not a confirmed booking. Wednesday noon is the requested scheduling-update time, not a work-completion deadline. Friday is the customer event. Keep these three time references separate when summarizing the conversation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The planned outdoor work is paused because of the site conditions after rain. / I understand the Friday event makes the timing important. / The office has suggested Thursday. / Crew availability has not been confirmed. / Thursday is a possibility, not a booked return visit. / Friday is your event date, not a completion promise from us. / I will pass the Friday event detail to the office. / I will request a scheduling update by Wednesday noon. / Wednesday noon concerns the update, not the work itself. / I cannot guarantee completion before the event. / The suggestion does not authorize work to resume now. / Availability still needs to be checked. / I am reporting the site conditions, not diagnosing a drainage fault. / Work is paused and no return date is confirmed. / Please do not treat Thursday as a confirmed arrangement yet. / I will relay your event deadline and the requested update time.
Vocabulary: weather pause: Temporary stop associated with weather-related conditions. / site conditions: Physical circumstances at the work location. / rainfall: Rain received at a location. / ground condition: State of the surface or soil at the site. / waterlogged: Saturated with water beyond normal drainage. / planned work: Tasks intended for the scheduled visit. / resumption: Starting work again after a pause. / tentative date: Proposed day that is not yet confirmed. / crew availability: Whether the required workers are available. / scheduling update: New information about arrangements for work. / return visit: Later attendance to continue or review work. / event deadline: Time constraint created by a planned event. / completion date: Day on which work is expected or agreed to finish. / weather dependency: Reliance on suitable weather-related circumstances. / provisional suggestion: Idea offered before arrangements are confirmed. / customer constraint: Limitation affecting what the customer needs. / office coordinator: Person arranging visits and communication. / confirmation status: Whether an arrangement is agreed or remains open. / update target: Requested time for the next communication. / service interruption: Break in planned service delivery. / revised schedule: Changed arrangement for when work occurs. / outstanding check: Matter still requiring verification. / deadline pressure: Urgency arising from a time limit. / work status: Current stage of the assigned work.
Speaking task: Read the three time references: Wednesday-noon update, suggested Thursday, Friday event. Repeat requested and suggested with emphasis; neither means confirmed. Read the current pause without implying the calendar suggestion authorizes resumption.
Partner: Joel: Customer / Imani: Grounds lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the customer update. Keep the pause, the suggested Thursday visit, the requested Wednesday-noon update, and the Friday event in their correct roles.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Joel: Please request Wednesday noon and flag Friday. I won't tell the organizer Thursday is booked.
Imani: I will pass both details to the office coordinator. Requesting the update does not mean the office has already accepted the deadline, so I will describe it accurately.
Joel: Thanks. Work paused, Thursday suggested, update requested by Wednesday noon, event Friday. I'll keep those separate.
Imani: That is the correct summary of the service interruption. I will relay it without adding a booking or a promise that everything will be finished before your event.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Speaking task: Read the three time references: Wednesday-noon update, suggested Thursday, Friday event. Repeat requested and suggested with emphasis; neither means confirmed. Read the current pause without implying the calendar suggestion authorizes resumption.
Partner: Joel: Customer / Imani: Grounds lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the customer update. Keep the pause, the suggested Thursday visit, the requested Wednesday-noon update, and the Friday event in their correct roles.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Joel: Please request Wednesday noon and flag Friday. I won't tell the organizer Thursday is booked.
Imani: I will pass both details to the office coordinator. Requesting the update does not mean the office has already accepted the deadline, so I will describe it accurately.
Joel: Thanks. Work paused, Thursday suggested, update requested by Wednesday noon, event Friday. I'll keep those separate.
Imani: That is the correct summary of the service interruption. I will relay it without adding a booking or a promise that everything will be finished before your event.
END REFERENCE
05Reporting site observations without diagnosisStanding water beside the east bed+

Your goal

Report a visible condition with its location and time, and respond to a suspected cause without presenting it as confirmed.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Standing water beside the east bed

At 09:15, worker Leila sees standing water beside the east bed near irrigation zone 3 at Birch Court. Customer Evan asks whether a pipe is broken. The cause is unknown, and no irrigation-system check has occurred. Leila can report the observation and refer it to grounds lead Marco, who can arrange review. She must not turn the location near a zone into proof that the zone, a pipe, or another component has failed.

Evan: Customer / Leila: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Leila observe?

Answer and explanation

D. Standing water beside the east bed at 09:15

The observation is standing water at the stated place and time, not a confirmed fault.

2. What has not occurred?

Answer and explanation

B. An irrigation-system check

The case explicitly says no system check has occurred.

3. Who can arrange review?

Answer and explanation

A. Grounds lead Marco

Marco is the lead who can arrange review, but no repair appointment is confirmed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Standing water is an observation. A broken pipe is a possible explanation raised by Evan, not a finding. Near zone 3 identifies the location; it does not prove that zone 3 or any particular irrigation component is faulty.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
standing water
Water collected on a surface rather than visibly draining away.report standing water
pooling
Accumulation of water in a particular area.describe observed pooling
irrigation
Artificial supply of water to plants or land.discuss the irrigation system
irrigation zone
Defined area or circuit served as part of an irrigation system.identify irrigation zone 3
sprinkler head
Outlet that distributes irrigation water.identify a sprinkler head
irrigation line
Pipe or tubing carrying water within an irrigation system.refer to an irrigation line
valve
Component controlling flow through a line.identify a valve
controller
Device managing system operation settings or timing.refer to the controller
drainage
Movement or removal of water from an area.describe a drainage concern
runoff
Water flowing over a surface.report visible runoff
saturation
Condition of material holding a high amount of water.describe visible saturation
east bed
Planting area on the east side of the identified site.locate the east bed
observation time
When the reported condition was seen.include the observation time
visible condition
State that can be directly seen.report the visible condition
suspected cause
Possible explanation that has not been established.distinguish a suspected cause
confirmed fault
Problem established through appropriate checking.avoid claiming a confirmed fault
system check
Examination of the relevant system.request a system check
leak
Escape of water from a system or container.refer a possible leak concern
pipework
Pipes collectively within a system.refer to the pipework
inspection request
Request for examination of a condition or system.pass an inspection request
precipitation rate
Irrigation application depth per unit of time, commonly millimeters or inches per hour.compare precipitation rates
review owner
Person responsible for arranging or carrying out a review.name the review owner
diagnosis
Identification of the cause or nature of a problem.distinguish observation from diagnosis
distribution uniformity
Measure of how evenly irrigation is applied across sampled positions.assess distribution uniformity

The useful report includes what was seen, where, when, and what has not been checked. Marco can arrange review. Do not add a leak diagnosis, repair plan, visit time, or claim that someone has already tested the system.

C Notice the language

Answer the concern without adopting the diagnosis

A customer may offer a possible explanation in the form of a question. Acknowledge the concern, then separate what you saw from what has not been checked. Specific location and time details make the referral useful without requiring a guessed technical answer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the observation
I saw standing water beside the east bed.
Give the time
I noticed it at 09:15.
Locate the area
It is near irrigation zone 3.
Acknowledge the concern
I understand why you are asking about a pipe.
Separate possibility from finding
A broken pipe has not been confirmed.
State the limit
I do not know the cause.
Keep the check status clear
No irrigation-system check has taken place.
Avoid a location inference
Being near zone 3 does not identify the faulty component.
Refer the observation
I will report what I saw to Marco.
Name the review route
The grounds lead can arrange a review.
Preserve the customer question
I will include your question about a possible pipe problem.
Avoid a repair promise
No repair or visit time is confirmed.
Distinguish reporting and testing
Passing the report does not mean the system has been tested.
Use neutral wording
Standing water observed; cause unknown.
Read back the details
East bed, near zone 3, observed at 09:15.
Close with the next step
Marco can review the report and arrange the appropriate follow-up.
6 language and precision notes
Observed
Claims only that the worker saw the condition.
Near
Locates the observation without establishing a causal connection.
Could be
Introduces a possibility and should not be repeated later as a confirmed cause.
No check yet
Preserves the actual evidence limit at the time of the conversation.
Arrange review
Describes a next step, not a completed inspection or repair.
Cause unknown
Gives a precise limit rather than a vague reassurance.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Standing water beside the east bed near zone 3 at 09:15; cause unknown.

Only the visible water, location, time, and unknown cause are established.

2. How should Leila answer the pipe question?

Answer and explanation

B. I cannot confirm that; no system check has occurred.

The response preserves uncertainty without asserting or excluding an untested cause.

3. Which detail must not be silently changed?

Answer and explanation

A. Near zone 3 must not become a confirmed fault in zone 3.

A location reference does not establish that the nearby system zone caused the water.

4. Which next step is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Refer the observation to Marco, who can arrange review.

The lead can arrange review, while inspection findings and repair arrangements remain unknown.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

Standing water beside the east bed

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Evan: Customer / Leila: Grounds worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • standing water
  • suspected cause
  • review owner
  • system check
  • inspection request
  • irrigation zone
  • observation time
  • visible condition
  • site location
  • confirmed fault
  1. Evan
    There's water beside the east bed. Could a pipe be broken? I'm worried it might be more than a wet patch.
  2. Leila
    I noticed the 1 at nine fifteen. I can report exactly where it is, but I haven't established what caused it.
  3. Evan
    Is that near zone 3 on the irrigation plan? I remember seeing that number beside this part of the garden.
  4. Leila
    Yes, near 2 3. That gives us a location reference; it doesn't prove that the zone or a particular component caused the water.
  5. Evan
    Has anyone checked the system? I don't want to tell the office it's a pipe problem if they've already found something else.
  6. Leila
    No 3 has happened yet. I'll include that in the report so it isn't mistaken for an inspection result.
  7. Evan
    Please include the time you saw it. The patch may look different by the time someone comes to review it.
  8. Leila
    I'll record the 4 as zero nine fifteen, with the east bed and nearby zone number. It isn't a confirmed fault-start time.
  9. Evan
    I'm asking about a pipe, not saying I know one's broken. That was just the first explanation that occurred to me.
  10. Leila
    I'll keep it as a 5 raised in your question. The observation itself is standing water, with the cause still unknown.
  11. Evan
    Who should get the report? I'd like someone to take the next step rather than have it passed around without an owner.
  12. Leila
    Marco, the grounds lead, is the 6 for this referral. He can arrange the appropriate review of what you've raised.
  13. Evan
    So we're asking for it to be checked. That doesn't mean there's already a repair crew booked to come here?
  14. Leila
    Correct. We're reporting a 7 and requesting review. No repair or attendance time has been confirmed.
  15. Evan
    Could the note actually say cause unknown? I don't want a message about a possible leak shortened to leak confirmed.
  16. Leila
    Yes. I won't record a 8. I'll retain cause unknown and no system check, alongside what I actually saw.
  17. Evan
    Read the location back once, please. There are several beds, and I want the lead sent the right information.
  18. Leila
    The 9 is beside the east bed, near zone 3, observed at zero nine fifteen. Your question concerns a possible pipe problem.
  19. Evan
    That's right. Please pass it to Marco with the time and the fact that nothing has been checked yet.
  20. Leila
    I'll send that 10. Marco can arrange review, and any later finding must come from the actual check, not our guess about the cause.

Answers and explanations
  1. standing water Turn 2. Standing water names the visible condition without identifying the cause or proving a pipe failure.
  2. irrigation zone Turn 4. Irrigation zone identifies the nearby numbered area; proximity does not establish that the zone is faulty.
  3. system check Turn 6. System check has not occurred, so no test result or confirmed cause can be included in the report.
  4. observation time Turn 8. Observation time is 09:15, when Leila saw the water; it is not a failure time or inspection appointment.
  5. suspected cause Turn 10. Suspected cause preserves Evan's pipe question as a possibility rather than a diagnosis or established finding.
  6. review owner Turn 12. Review owner identifies Marco as the person who can arrange review of the reported condition.
  7. visible condition Turn 14. Visible condition is the water Leila observed; reporting it does not establish a repair booking or technical conclusion.
  8. confirmed fault Turn 16. Confirmed fault would claim a problem had been established through checking; no such finding exists in the case.
  9. site location Turn 18. Site location is beside the east bed near zone 3, which distinguishes this observation from other planting beds.
  10. inspection request Turn 20. Inspection request asks for review of the observation; it does not mean an inspection or repair has already happened.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Leila: I noticed the standing water at nine fifteen. I can report exactly where it is, but I haven't established what caused it.
  3. Evan: Is that near zone 3 on the irrigation plan? I remember seeing that number beside this part of the garden.

  5. Evan: Has anyone checked the system? I don't want to tell the office it's a pipe problem if they've already found something else.
  6. Leila: No system check has happened yet. I'll include that in the report so it isn't mistaken for an inspection result.
  7. Evan: Please include the time you saw it. The patch may look different by the time someone comes to review it.
  8. Leila: I'll record the observation time as zero nine fifteen, with the east bed and nearby zone number. It isn't a confirmed fault-start time.
  9. Evan: I'm asking about a pipe, not saying I know one's broken. That was just the first explanation that occurred to me.

  11. Evan: Who should get the report? I'd like someone to take the next step rather than have it passed around without an owner.
  12. Leila: Marco, the grounds lead, is the review owner for this referral. He can arrange the appropriate review of what you've raised.
  13. Evan: So we're asking for it to be checked. That doesn't mean there's already a repair crew booked to come here?
  14. Leila: Correct. We're reporting a visible condition and requesting review. No repair or attendance time has been confirmed.

Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Water near zone three

A worker reports a site observation to a customer.

  1. Worker: At 09:15, I saw standing water beside the east bed, near irrigation zone three.
  2. Customer: Does that mean an underground pipe is broken, or could the recent rain explain it?
  3. Worker: I haven't checked the irrigation system, so I can't confirm either explanation from this observation.
  4. Customer: Then please tell the grounds lead exactly where you saw it and when it was visible.
  5. Worker: I'll report the east bed, zone three, and nine fifteen, with the cause explicitly unconfirmed.
  6. Customer: Our neighbor says that area always pools after rain. Should you include what they said?
  7. Worker: Yes. I'll note that your neighbor reports pooling after rain, but I haven't verified that history.
  8. Customer: That sounds fair. I don't want a note saying we've already diagnosed a pipe problem.
  9. Worker: I'll keep the visible pooling separate from the possible causes and request the lead's review.
  10. Customer: Please identify the review contact without promising a repair outcome.
A bare patch does not identify a treatment

A customer asks a grounds worker about a lawn observation.

  1. Customer: Which treatment would fix this bare patch beside the walkway?
  2. Worker: I can report the patch and its location, but I can't diagnose its cause or prescribe a treatment.
  3. Customer: It seems larger than last week, although I didn't measure it or take a dated photograph.
  4. Worker: I'll describe that as your impression of change, alongside what is visible during today's visit.
  5. Customer: Please don't write that it doubled in size. I don't have evidence for an exact amount.
  6. Worker: I won't. The report will distinguish your observation over time from a measured comparison we haven't made.
  7. Customer: Who can review the plant-health concern and identify useful assessment information?
  8. Worker: The qualified grounds lead can take that question and discuss an appropriate review with you.
  9. Customer: Then the immediate result is a clear report, not an assumption that you have started a treatment.
  10. Worker: I'll relay today's observation and your qualified account of the change.
An observation was assigned to the wrong bed

A worker corrects a site-report location with the grounds lead.

  1. Worker: My yellowing-leaf report named the north bed; I meant east.
  2. Grounds lead: Thanks for catching that. Does the correction change only the location, or also what you observed?
  3. Worker: Only the location. I saw yellowing on several leaves; I haven't identified a disease or cause.
  4. Grounds lead: Then please preserve the observation and correct the bed name without adding a diagnosis to the note.
  5. Worker: The east bed is beside the reception window, not the planting area by the north gate.
  6. Grounds lead: That landmark helps. I'll use it when arranging the review so the right area is assessed.
  7. Worker: Should I notify the office, which received my incorrect location earlier?
  8. Grounds lead: Yes. Updating the note alone doesn't establish that everyone who read the earlier version has seen the correction.
  9. Worker: I'll contact the office with the corrected location and say the cause remains unconfirmed.
  10. Grounds lead: Yes. That corrects the location without making an unsupported plant-health diagnosis.

  2. Repeat the possible-pipe question as a question, not a confirmed diagnosis.

Answer and explanation

B. 09:15

This is when Leila saw the condition, not when a fault began.

3. Worker: "The area is near zone ___."

Answer and explanation

Zone 3 is the location reference, not proof of a faulty zone.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I saw standing water beside the east bed. / I noticed it at 09:15. / It is near irrigation zone 3. / I understand why you are asking about a pipe. / A broken pipe has not been confirmed. / I do not know the cause. / No irrigation-system check has taken place. / Being near zone 3 does not identify the faulty component. / I will report what I saw to Marco. / The grounds lead can arrange a review. / I will include your question about a possible pipe problem. / No repair or visit time is confirmed. / Passing the report does not mean the system has been tested. / Standing water observed; cause unknown. / East bed, near zone 3, observed at 09:15. / Marco can review the report and arrange the appropriate follow-up.
Vocabulary: standing water: Water collected on a surface rather than visibly draining away. / pooling: Accumulation of water in a particular area. / irrigation: Artificial supply of water to plants or land. / irrigation zone: Defined area or circuit served as part of an irrigation system. / sprinkler head: Outlet that distributes irrigation water. / irrigation line: Pipe or tubing carrying water within an irrigation system. / valve: Component controlling flow through a line. / controller: Device managing system operation settings or timing. / drainage: Movement or removal of water from an area. / runoff: Water flowing over a surface. / saturation: Condition of material holding a high amount of water. / east bed: Planting area on the east side of the identified site. / observation time: When the reported condition was seen. / visible condition: State that can be directly seen. / suspected cause: Possible explanation that has not been established. / confirmed fault: Problem established through appropriate checking. / system check: Examination of the relevant system. / leak: Escape of water from a system or container. / pipework: Pipes collectively within a system. / inspection request: Request for examination of a condition or system. / precipitation rate: Irrigation application depth per unit of time, commonly millimeters or inches per hour. / review owner: Person responsible for arranging or carrying out a review. / diagnosis: Identification of the cause or nature of a problem. / distribution uniformity: Measure of how evenly irrigation is applied across sampled positions.
Speaking task: Read standing water, east bed, near zone 3, and 09:15. Repeat the possible-pipe question as a question, not a confirmed diagnosis. Read Marco's role and leave inspection findings and visit timing unresolved.
Partner: Evan: Customer / Leila: Grounds worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the report to Marco. Keep the visible condition, time, location, and unknown cause distinct. Do not turn the customer question into a diagnosis.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Evan: Read the location back once, please. There are several beds, and I want the lead sent the right information.
Leila: The site location is beside the east bed, near zone 3, observed at zero nine fifteen. Your question concerns a possible pipe problem.
Evan: That's right. Please pass it to Marco with the time and the fact that nothing has been checked yet.
Leila: I'll send that inspection request. Marco can arrange review, and any later finding must come from the actual check, not our guess about the cause.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 05: Reporting site observations without diagnosis
Original fictional case: At 09:15, worker Leila sees standing water beside the east bed near irrigation zone 3 at Birch Court. Customer Evan asks whether a pipe is broken. The cause is unknown, and no irrigation-system check has occurred. Leila can report the observation and refer it to grounds lead Marco, who can arrange review. She must not turn the location near a zone into proof that the zone, a pipe, or another component has failed.
