06Checking materials and requested quantitiesSix bags and the wrong label+

Your goal

Compare a delivery with the requested quantity and approved sample, then refer both discrepancies without approving substitution.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Six bags and the wrong label

At Cedar Lane, a front-bed job lists eight bags of dark mulch. Customer Mira approved a dark sample. Worker Dev counts six delivered bags, whose label says natural brown. The delivered material has not been approved as a substitute. Dev cannot promise that six bags will cover the beds or that the labeled color matches the sample. The office will query the supplier about both the two-bag shortage and the apparent color discrepancy.

Mira: Customer / Dev: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What quantity is listed and what arrived?

Answer and explanation

D. Eight bags listed; six delivered; two short

Subtracting six delivered bags from eight listed bags gives a two-bag shortage.

2. Which description raises a second question?

Answer and explanation

D. Natural brown on the delivered label versus the approved dark sample

The delivered label differs from the requested dark description and approved sample.

3. What is the next step?

Answer and explanation

D. Office query to the supplier about both discrepancies

The office will query both quantity and description without assuming substitution or coverage approval.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Eight requested minus six delivered leaves two bags short. Separately, natural brown appears on the delivered label while the job and approved sample specify dark mulch. The quantity calculation does not resolve whether the delivered material matches or is acceptable.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
mulch
Material placed over a soil surface for a specified landscape purpose.confirm the mulch specification
bag count
Number of bags counted in a delivery.verify the bag count
requested quantity
Amount stated in the job or order.compare the requested quantity
delivered quantity
Amount actually received.record the delivered quantity
shortfall
Amount by which the delivered total is below the required total.report a two-bag shortfall
material specification
Stated requirements for a material.check the material specification
approved sample
Example accepted as a reference for the requested product.compare the approved sample
color description
Words identifying the intended or labeled color.check the color description
natural brown
Color description on the delivered bags in this case.record the natural-brown label
dark mulch
Requested mulch description in the job.confirm the dark-mulch request
product label
Information attached to or printed on the material packaging.read the product label
supplier query
Question sent to the business providing the material.raise a supplier query
quantity discrepancy
Difference between expected and actual amounts.report a quantity discrepancy
description discrepancy
Difference between the requested and delivered descriptions.report a description discrepancy
substitute material
Product proposed instead of the specified material.seek approval for substitute material
substitution approval
Agreement to use an alternative product.confirm substitution approval
coverage
Area that a quantity of material can cover under relevant conditions.avoid an unsupported coverage promise
material takeoff
Calculation of required material quantities from dimensions and specifications.check the material takeoff
delivery record
Document or entry describing a delivery.check the delivery record
order reference
Identifier connecting a query to the relevant order.include the order reference
replacement request
Request for an appropriate replacement product.discuss a replacement request
net area
Area requiring material after stated exclusions have been subtracted.calculate the net area
acceptance
Agreement that delivered material meets the relevant requirement.distinguish receipt from acceptance
material match
Agreement between a supplied material and its specified reference.verify the material match

The case does not supply bed dimensions, application requirements, or a verified coverage calculation. Do not promise that six bags will be enough. Neither physical delivery nor a customer question about using it establishes approval of substitute material.

C Notice the language

Separate two discrepancies instead of averaging them away

A delivery can have more than one mismatch. State the count and the description separately, and keep each compared with its correct reference. A customer question about making do does not establish coverage, color acceptance, or authority to substitute the material.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the order quantity
The job lists eight bags.
State the delivery count
I have counted six delivered bags.
Calculate the difference
That leaves a two-bag shortfall.
Name the requested material
The specified material is dark mulch.
Read the delivered description
The delivered label says natural brown.
Use the sample reference
Your approved sample was dark.
Keep the issues separate
We have a quantity question and a material-description question.
Avoid a match claim
I cannot confirm that this matches the approved sample.
Avoid a coverage claim
I cannot promise that six bags will cover the beds.
Preserve approval status
No substitution has been approved.
Refer both discrepancies
The office will query the supplier about both points.
Avoid changing the order
I am not recording six bags as the requested quantity.
Avoid assigning blame
The cause of the discrepancy has not been established.
Keep timing open
No replacement delivery time is confirmed.
Read back the comparison
Eight requested, six delivered, and natural brown on the label.
Close with the review route
The supplier query needs to resolve quantity and material before acceptance is assumed.
6 language and precision notes
Short by two
Expresses the difference between eight expected and six received.
Label says
Reports packaging information without claiming a verified visual or technical match.
Approved sample
Refers to the customer's accepted reference, not any later substitute.
Enough
A coverage claim requiring more information than the bag count alone.
Both
Keeps quantity and material description in the supplier query.
Delivered versus accepted
Receiving material does not establish that it satisfies the order.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which calculation is correct?

Answer and explanation

B. Eight requested minus six delivered equals two bags short.

Comparing the expected and received counts shows a shortage of two bags.

2. Which statement preserves the color issue?

Answer and explanation

C. The label says natural brown, while the approved sample was dark.

The statement compares the actual label with the approved reference without claiming a match.

3. How should Dev answer whether six bags are enough?

Answer and explanation

A. I cannot promise coverage from the information we have.

The case lacks the measurements and application information needed for a coverage promise.

4. What belongs in the supplier query?

Answer and explanation

D. Both the two-bag shortage and the material-description discrepancy

Both unresolved differences need review; neither acceptance nor replacement timing is established.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Six bags and the wrong label

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mira: Customer / Dev: Grounds worker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • approved sample
  • requested quantity
  • replacement request
  • acceptance
  • coverage
  • product label
  • quantity discrepancy
  • Substitution approval
  • bag count
  • supplier query
  1. Mira
    We expected eight bags of dark mulch. I'm counting fewer, and these labels say natural brown. Can you check?
  2. Dev
    I have checked the 1 and found six. The job lists eight bags, so the delivery is two bags short before we address the description.
  3. Mira
    My approved sample was dark. Does natural brown mean someone changed the specification, or don't we know yet?
  4. Dev
    I cannot say that. The 2 says natural brown, and the requested material is dark mulch. No approved change has been established.
  5. Mira
    If the color is acceptable, could six bags cover the beds? Or are we short of what the job needs?
  6. Dev
    I cannot promise 3 from the count alone. We need to keep the missing quantity separate from the question of whether this material matches what you approved.
  7. Mira
    Please keep eight as the ordered quantity. Six is what arrived, not what I asked for.
  8. Dev
    I will preserve the 4 as eight. The record will show six delivered and two short, not a revised order for six bags.
  9. Mira
    Could you include the actual sample in the query? The color word alone isn't the reference I approved.
  10. Dev
    I will refer to the 5 as well as the job description. I am not confirming a match between that sample and the delivered bags.
  11. Mira
    What happens next? I don't want the crew using it on the assumption that it's close enough.
  12. Dev
    The office will raise a 6 about both points. The query will include the two-bag shortage and the difference between the label and your approved reference.
  13. Mira
    Are we ordering a replacement now, or just asking the supplier to explain the delivery?
  14. Dev
    No 7 has been confirmed as an order. I also do not have a replacement delivery time to give you now.
  15. Mira
    All right. I'm asking about options, not accepting a smaller quantity or a different material.
  16. Dev
    I will. 8 is still absent. Your question about making do will not be recorded as acceptance of a different material or a smaller quantity.
  17. Mira
    Can you summarize both issues? I don't want the missing two bags to hide the color question.
  18. Dev
    The 9 is eight requested against six delivered. Separately, the delivered label says natural brown while your approved sample and the job request are dark.
  19. Mira
    Please send both points. We still don't know whether the material matches or whether six bags could cover the beds.
  20. Dev
    I will keep 10 open while the office queries the supplier. The count, the material description, and any proposed resolution will remain separate in the message.

Answers and explanations
  1. bag count Turn 2. Bag count is six delivered bags, which must be compared with the eight bags listed in the job.
  2. product label Turn 4. Product label supplies the delivered description natural brown; it does not explain why that material was delivered.
  3. coverage Turn 6. Coverage depends on information not supplied here; six bags cannot be declared sufficient simply because they arrived.
  4. requested quantity Turn 8. Requested quantity remains eight bags; receiving six does not amend the original job requirement.
  5. approved sample Turn 10. Approved sample is the dark reference Mira accepted; the delivered label has not been verified against it as a match.
  6. supplier query Turn 12. Supplier query is the agreed next route for investigating both quantity and material description, without assuming acceptance.
  7. replacement request Turn 14. Replacement request is not yet confirmed as an order or delivery arrangement; the current step is a supplier query.
  8. Substitution approval Turn 16. Substitution approval has not been given; asking about possibilities does not authorize an alternative material.
  9. quantity discrepancy Turn 18. Quantity discrepancy is two bags short, while the material-description discrepancy remains a separate unresolved issue.
  10. acceptance Turn 20. Acceptance of the delivery is not established; the office must resolve the quantity and material questions through the supplier.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mira: We expected eight bags of dark mulch. I'm counting fewer, and these labels say natural brown. Can you check?
  2. Dev: I have checked the bag count and found six. The job lists eight bags, so the delivery is two bags short before we address the description.
  3. Mira: My approved sample was dark. Does natural brown mean someone changed the specification, or don't we know yet?

  8. Dev: I will preserve the requested quantity as eight. The record will show six delivered and two short, not a revised order for six bags.
  9. Mira: Could you include the actual sample in the query? The color word alone isn't the reference I approved.
  10. Dev: I will refer to the approved sample as well as the job description. I am not confirming a match between that sample and the delivered bags.
  11. Mira: What happens next? I don't want the crew using it on the assumption that it's close enough.
  12. Dev: The office will raise a supplier query about both points. The query will include the two-bag shortage and the difference between the label and your approved reference.
  13. Mira: Are we ordering a replacement now, or just asking the supplier to explain the delivery?
  14. Dev: No replacement request has been confirmed as an order. I also do not have a replacement delivery time to give you now.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Six bags in a different color

A worker discusses a material discrepancy with the office.

  1. Worker: Eight dark-mulch bags were ordered; six natural-brown bags arrived.
  2. Office clerk: Does the material match the approved sample, or is the quantity the only difference?
  3. Worker: The labels say natural brown, while the customer's approved material sample was described as dark.
  4. Office clerk: Then we need to query both quantity and material description before saying the delivery matches.
  5. Worker: I haven't approved a substitute or told the customer that six bags cover the intended area.
  6. Office clerk: Good. A bag count alone doesn't establish the coverage area or the appropriate application for this job.
  7. Worker: Please query the front-bed work order; rear beds are excluded.
  8. Office clerk: I'll compare that order with the sample record and ask about the two missing bags separately.
  9. Worker: What should I tell the customer while those two discrepancies remain under review?
  10. Office clerk: Report both differences, with no substitute approved while the review remains open.
The sample is not the entire delivery

A customer asks a worker about materials arriving for review.

  1. Customer: One small bag arrived; wasn't material ordered for both beds?
  2. Worker: This is labeled material sample for review, not the full delivery quantity for the job.
  3. Customer: I see. I mistook its arrival for the main delivery because both messages mentioned the same work order.
  4. Worker: The shared reference links it to your job, but the shipment description identifies its purpose as a sample.
  5. Customer: Then approving its appearance wouldn't by itself confirm how much material the beds require?
  6. Worker: Correct. The quantity and coverage area still need their own confirmation from the responsible lead and office.
  7. Customer: I'd like to compare both samples before choosing the material.
  8. Worker: I'll pass that preference on and keep the material choice described as pending your review.
  9. Customer: Please don't schedule work just because the sample arrived at the property today.
  10. Worker: I'll clarify: sample received, choice pending, main delivery and work timing unconfirmed.
Front beds, not the whole garden

A worker clarifies the area covered by a material estimate.

  1. Worker: The estimate covers two front beds; your message says whole garden.
  2. Customer: I used garden loosely. I meant the front beds beside the path, not the rear lawn.
  3. Worker: Thanks. Does that include the small bed around the entrance sign, or only the two long beds?
  4. Customer: Only the two long beds. The sign bed has separate planting that we don't want included.
  5. Worker: Then I'll keep the coverage area limited to those two front beds when the office checks the quantity.
  6. Customer: Please do. I don't want a larger material order based on an imprecise description in my message.
  7. Worker: Has the office confirmed a quantity, or are we clarifying the area?
  8. Customer: We're clarifying the area. I haven't approved any revised quantity or additional charge.
  9. Worker: I'll report that distinction so the area clarification isn't mistaken for an approved change to the order.
  10. Customer: Exactly. Confirm the area before discussing the required delivery quantity.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Six bags and the wrong label
  1. Read eight requested, six delivered, and two bags short.
  2. Compare natural brown on the label with the approved dark sample.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report two delivery discrepancies

Complete the office handoff. Include the original quantity, the delivered count, the resulting shortage, and the unapproved material description.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The job requires ___ bags of dark mulch."

Answer and explanation

D. eight

Eight is the requested quantity and remains unchanged by the delivery.

2. Worker: "We received ___ bags."

Answer and explanation

B. six

Six is the delivered count, not a revised order quantity.

3. Worker: "We are ___ bags short."

Answer and explanation

C. two

Eight requested minus six delivered leaves a shortage of two bags.

4. Worker: "The label says natural ___; substitution is not approved."

Answer and explanation

B. brown

Natural brown is the delivered description, which differs from the dark reference.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Communication goal: Compare a delivery with the requested quantity and approved sample, then refer both discrepancies without approving substitution.
Language guidance: Eight requested minus six delivered leaves two bags short. Separately, natural brown appears on the delivered label while the job and approved sample specify dark mulch. The quantity calculation does not resolve whether the delivered material matches or is acceptable.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The job lists eight bags. / I have counted six delivered bags. / That leaves a two-bag shortfall. / The specified material is dark mulch. / The delivered label says natural brown. / Your approved sample was dark. / We have a quantity question and a material-description question. / I cannot confirm that this matches the approved sample. / I cannot promise that six bags will cover the beds. / No substitution has been approved. / The office will query the supplier about both points. / I am not recording six bags as the requested quantity. / The cause of the discrepancy has not been established. / No replacement delivery time is confirmed. / Eight requested, six delivered, and natural brown on the label. / The supplier query needs to resolve quantity and material before acceptance is assumed.
Vocabulary: mulch: Material placed over a soil surface for a specified landscape purpose. / bag count: Number of bags counted in a delivery. / requested quantity: Amount stated in the job or order. / delivered quantity: Amount actually received. / shortfall: Amount by which the delivered total is below the required total. / material specification: Stated requirements for a material. / approved sample: Example accepted as a reference for the requested product. / color description: Words identifying the intended or labeled color. / natural brown: Color description on the delivered bags in this case. / dark mulch: Requested mulch description in the job. / product label: Information attached to or printed on the material packaging. / supplier query: Question sent to the business providing the material. / quantity discrepancy: Difference between expected and actual amounts. / description discrepancy: Difference between the requested and delivered descriptions. / substitute material: Product proposed instead of the specified material. / substitution approval: Agreement to use an alternative product. / coverage: Area that a quantity of material can cover under relevant conditions. / material takeoff: Calculation of required material quantities from dimensions and specifications. / delivery record: Document or entry describing a delivery. / order reference: Identifier connecting a query to the relevant order. / replacement request: Request for an appropriate replacement product. / net area: Area requiring material after stated exclusions have been subtracted. / acceptance: Agreement that delivered material meets the relevant requirement. / material match: Agreement between a supplied material and its specified reference.
Speaking task: Read eight requested, six delivered, and two bags short. Compare natural brown on the label with the approved dark sample. Read the supplier query with both discrepancies and no coverage or substitution promise.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Responding to finish and cleanup concernsClippings beside the front steps+

Your goal

Acknowledge a specific missed cleanup, identify the included area, and refer correction without expanding the job.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Clippings beside the front steps

After a front-lawn visit at Rowan Court, customer Hazel says the path cleanup was incomplete. Worker Amir can see clippings beside the front steps, which are within the listed cleanup area. The rear patio is excluded from the booking. The lead can organize the missed front cleanup, but no wider task or exact return time is agreed. Amir must acknowledge the visible concern without denying it, declaring it resolved, or adding the rear patio to the job.

Hazel: Customer / Amir: Grounds worker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What can Amir see?

Answer and explanation

C. Clippings beside the front steps

The worker can see clippings in the front-step area identified by the customer.

2. Which area is included?

Answer and explanation

C. The front steps within the listed cleanup area

The front steps fall within the listed cleanup area, while the rear patio is excluded.

3. What can the lead organize?

Answer and explanation

A. The missed front cleanup

The lead can organize correction of the missed included work without adding wider tasks.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The visible clippings support Hazel regarding the missed front cleanup. They do not establish a wider problem across every area of the property. The front steps are included; the rear patio is excluded. Correcting the included task does not automatically expand the booking.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
clippings
Small pieces of plant material left after cutting.report remaining clippings
cleanup area
Location included in the agreed clearing task.identify the cleanup area
front steps
Steps at the front entrance of the property.locate the front steps
rear patio
Paved outdoor area at the back of the property.distinguish the rear patio
missed cleanup
Included clearing work left incomplete.acknowledge missed cleanup
finish concern
Customer concern about the final condition of work.record a finish concern
visible residue
Material remaining that can be seen.describe visible residue
incomplete task
Assigned work that has not been fully carried out.identify the incomplete task
corrective visit
Later attendance intended to address a problem.discuss a corrective visit
remedial work
Work intended to correct an identified issue.arrange remedial work
included area
Location covered by the booking.confirm the included area
excluded area
Location outside the agreed booking.explain the excluded area
customer complaint
Expression of dissatisfaction with a service or result.acknowledge a customer complaint
service recovery
Action to address a problem in delivered service.discuss service recovery
completion status
Whether the relevant work is finished.correct the completion status
scope boundary
Limit of the work or area agreed.preserve the scope boundary
follow-up arrangement
Plan for the next contact or attendance.confirm a follow-up arrangement
return time
Time of a later attendance.avoid inventing a return time
cleanup standard
Agreed expectation for the condition after clearing.refer to the cleanup standard
workmanship
Quality of work performed.discuss a workmanship concern
correction request
Request to put an identified problem right.pass a correction request
additional area
Location beyond the existing task area.identify an additional area
observation record
Note of a condition directly seen.make an observation record
resolution
Outcome that addresses the reported issue.verify the resolution

The lead can organize the missed front cleanup, but organization is not completion. No precise return time is supplied. Avoid marking the complaint resolved before the correction is verified, or promising rear-patio work as part of the existing arrangement.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the specific problem before discussing scope

A customer who reports incomplete work needs evidence that the concern has been heard. Name the visible missed area first. Then separate the correction of an included task from a new request, keeping the explanation calm and specific rather than defensive.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
I can see the clippings beside the front steps.
Confirm the included area
Those steps are within the listed cleanup area.
State the missed work
That part of the cleanup is incomplete.
Avoid a denial
I will not describe the area as fully cleared.
Refer correction
The lead can organize the missed front cleanup.
Keep completion accurate
The correction has not yet been completed.
Avoid a timing promise
I do not have a confirmed return time.
Separate the rear area
The rear patio is outside this booking.
Preserve the scope
The correction concerns the included front area.
Acknowledge another request
Rear-patio cleanup would be a separate request.
Avoid generalizing
I am reporting this visible area, not claiming every task was missed.
Keep the record specific
Clippings remain beside the front steps.
Explain the next step
I will pass the correction request to the lead.
Avoid premature closure
I will not mark the concern resolved now.
Read back the handoff
Missed front cleanup, within scope, with follow-up still to be arranged.
Close respectfully
Thank you for showing me the exact area.
6 language and precision notes
I can see
Acknowledges direct evidence without requiring the customer to repeat the complaint.
That part
Limits the finding to the identified incomplete area.
Within scope
Connects the correction to the agreed booking.
Separate request
Keeps the excluded patio distinct from correction of the front cleanup.
Organize versus complete
Arranging a response is not evidence that the work is finished.
Resolved
Should describe an established outcome, not merely receipt of a complaint.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening best acknowledges the evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. I can see clippings beside the front steps, within the cleanup area.

The opening recognizes the directly visible problem in the included area.

2. Which scope statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. The lead can organize missed front cleanup; the rear patio remains excluded.

Correcting the included task does not change the excluded status of the rear patio.

3. Which status should not be recorded yet?

Answer and explanation

D. Resolved and fully corrected

No completed correction or verified resolution has occurred in the supplied facts.

4. What belongs in the correction request?

Answer and explanation

B. The exact front-step location, visible clippings, included scope, and pending follow-up

These details preserve the observed problem, its scope, and the unresolved arrangement.

Answers and explanations
  1. clippings Turn 2. Clippings are the visible remaining material beside the front steps, supporting the report of incomplete cleanup.
  2. completion status Turn 4. Completion status of the identified front cleanup is incomplete; leaving the site does not prove that task was finished.
  3. missed cleanup Turn 6. Missed cleanup is an included front-area task left incomplete, which the lead can arrange to address.
  4. rear patio Turn 8. Rear patio is excluded from this booking and does not become included through the front-cleanup correction request.
  5. scope boundary Turn 10. Scope boundary keeps the missed included front cleanup separate from the customer's additional rear-patio request.
  6. return time Turn 12. Return time is not supplied, so Amir cannot promise an exact attendance time for the correction.
  7. observation record Turn 14. Observation record preserves what Amir directly saw, rather than treating the concern as an unsupported general complaint.
  8. correction request Turn 16. Correction request concerns the specific missed front cleanup; it should not claim that every task or area was defective.
  9. Resolution Turn 18. Resolution requires the issue to be addressed; merely passing a message to the lead does not establish that outcome.
  10. follow-up arrangement Turn 20. Follow-up arrangement is still to be organized for the missed front cleanup; no wider task or exact time is agreed.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Clippings beside the front steps

A customer and worker review a missed part of the cleanup.

  1. Customer: Clippings remain at the front steps. Wasn't path cleanup included?
  2. Worker: You're right. That location is within the listed cleanup area, and I can see the clippings.
  3. Customer: Thank you for acknowledging it. I also wondered whether the rear patio should have been cleared.
  4. Worker: The rear patio is excluded from this visit. Let's separate that request from the missed front-step cleanup.
  5. Customer: I agree the front steps were already included. Who can arrange for that part to be completed?
  6. Worker: I'll ask the lead to organize it and confirm the plan, rather than invent a return time.
  7. Customer: Could we request a front-area walk-through with the lead?
  8. Worker: I'll relay that request. It will help compare the actual result with the agreed finish standard.
  9. Customer: Please preserve both points: missed front cleanup and a separate question about the rear patio.
  10. Worker: I'll preserve both issues, and I'm sorry the included cleanup was incomplete.
Debris from another contractor

A customer asks a grounds worker about a pile beside the job area.

  1. Customer: Does today's debris removal cover that pile beside the entrance?
  2. Worker: The work order includes clippings from our grounds tasks. Do you know where that separate pile came from?
  3. Customer: The paving contractor left it, but I assumed all outdoor waste would be included in your cleanup.
  4. Worker: I understand the assumption. Our listed removal doesn't identify that contractor's material, so the office needs to review it.
  5. Customer: Please don't describe the pile as your crew's missed cleanup if it wasn't generated by your work.
  6. Worker: I won't. I'll name it as a separate waste-removal request and pass on the source you reported.
  7. Customer: Can your team handle that material, or does it need review?
  8. Worker: That needs review by the lead. I can't identify or approve handling from this conversation alone.
  9. Customer: Then keep today's grounds cleanup separate while the office checks this additional request.
  10. Worker: I'll relay the separate request without promising handling, removal, or pricing.
Finished on one side, not both

A worker corrects an overbroad completion statement during a walk-through.

  1. Worker: I called the path finished, but its east edge remains uncleared.
  2. Customer: Yes, the west edge looks complete. I don't want the whole area described as untouched either.
  3. Worker: That's fair. I'll correct my statement to west edge complete, east edge still needing the listed clearance.
  4. Customer: Does that change the work order, or is it simply completing what was already agreed?
  5. Worker: It's the existing task, not a new request. The lead needs to confirm the completion arrangement.
  6. Customer: Please make sure the next person knows exactly which edge remains, so they don't repeat the completed part.
  7. Worker: I'll name the east edge beside the noticeboard, preserving the west as complete.
  8. Customer: That landmark is useful. 'The other side' could mean different things depending on where someone stands.
  9. Worker: I'll name that landmark and correct my premature statement that everything was finished.
  10. Customer: That gives us a precise finish check and a clear outstanding item.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Clippings beside the front steps
  1. Read the visible clippings and the included front-step location.
  2. Repeat the correction request separately from the excluded rear-patio request.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Acknowledge and refer the missed area

Complete the exchange about the visible front cleanup problem. Keep the included correction, excluded patio, and unresolved timing separate.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "Clippings remain beside the front ___."

Answer and explanation

B. steps

The steps are the exact location of the visible missed cleanup.

2. Customer: "That area was ___ in the booking."

Answer and explanation

C. included

The front-step area belongs to the listed cleanup scope.

3. Worker: "The rear patio remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. excluded

The patio is outside the booking and is not automatically added.

4. Worker: "The lead can organize correction; the return time is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

No exact return time is supplied, even though the lead can organize correction.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Responding to finish and cleanup concerns
Original fictional case: After a front-lawn visit at Rowan Court, customer Hazel says the path cleanup was incomplete. Worker Amir can see clippings beside the front steps, which are within the listed cleanup area. The rear patio is excluded from the booking. The lead can organize the missed front cleanup, but no wider task or exact return time is agreed. Amir must acknowledge the visible concern without denying it, declaring it resolved, or adding the rear patio to the job.
Language workshop: Clippings beside the front steps
Communication goal: Acknowledge a specific missed cleanup, identify the included area, and refer correction without expanding the job.
Language guidance: The visible clippings support Hazel regarding the missed front cleanup. They do not establish a wider problem across every area of the property. The front steps are included; the rear patio is excluded. Correcting the included task does not automatically expand the booking.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see the clippings beside the front steps. / Those steps are within the listed cleanup area. / That part of the cleanup is incomplete. / I will not describe the area as fully cleared. / The lead can organize the missed front cleanup. / The correction has not yet been completed. / I do not have a confirmed return time. / The rear patio is outside this booking. / The correction concerns the included front area. / Rear-patio cleanup would be a separate request. / I am reporting this visible area, not claiming every task was missed. / Clippings remain beside the front steps. / I will pass the correction request to the lead. / I will not mark the concern resolved now. / Missed front cleanup, within scope, with follow-up still to be arranged. / Thank you for showing me the exact area.
Vocabulary: clippings: Small pieces of plant material left after cutting. / cleanup area: Location included in the agreed clearing task. / front steps: Steps at the front entrance of the property. / rear patio: Paved outdoor area at the back of the property. / missed cleanup: Included clearing work left incomplete. / finish concern: Customer concern about the final condition of work. / visible residue: Material remaining that can be seen. / incomplete task: Assigned work that has not been fully carried out. / corrective visit: Later attendance intended to address a problem. / remedial work: Work intended to correct an identified issue. / included area: Location covered by the booking. / excluded area: Location outside the agreed booking. / customer complaint: Expression of dissatisfaction with a service or result. / service recovery: Action to address a problem in delivered service. / completion status: Whether the relevant work is finished. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or area agreed. / follow-up arrangement: Plan for the next contact or attendance. / return time: Time of a later attendance. / cleanup standard: Agreed expectation for the condition after clearing. / workmanship: Quality of work performed. / correction request: Request to put an identified problem right. / additional area: Location beyond the existing task area. / observation record: Note of a condition directly seen. / resolution: Outcome that addresses the reported issue.
Speaking task: Read the visible clippings and the included front-step location. Repeat the correction request separately from the excluded rear-patio request. Read the closing follow-up without marking the complaint resolved or inventing a time.
Partner: Hazel: Customer / Amir: Grounds worker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the exchange about the visible front cleanup problem. Keep the included correction, excluded patio, and unresolved timing separate.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hazel: Thanks. Please don't close the complaint just because we've spoken. I need the missed cleanup dealt with, not only acknowledged.
Amir: Resolution still needs to follow the actual correction. I'll leave it open rather than mark it resolved when I pass on the message.
Hazel: Good. The front steps are the first issue. The patio can be discussed separately, with its own scope and timing.
Amir: I'll ask the lead for the follow-up arrangement for the missed front cleanup. The exact return time remains unconfirmed until it's arranged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handing over unfinished grounds workA clear path and an open bed review+

Your goal

Separate completed work from a deferred review, preserve a callback request, and identify who owns the next arrangement.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

A clear path and an open bed review

At Pine Court, worker Rosa hands the visit over to office coordinator Ava. Front-path leaves are cleared. The lead deferred the rear-bed review; no planting or diagnosis was done there. The customer asks for a callback about the rear bed before Friday. Ava owns scheduling, but no visit date is booked. Rosa must distinguish the completed front-path task, the deferred review, the requested communication deadline, and the unresolved appointment.

Rosa: Grounds worker / Ava: Office coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which work is complete?

Answer and explanation

B. Front-path leaf clearance

The case confirms only that the front-path leaves are cleared.

2. What happened to the rear-bed review?

Answer and explanation

B. The lead deferred it; no planting or diagnosis was done

The lead deferred the review, leaving both planting and diagnosis unperformed.

3. What does the customer request?

Answer and explanation

B. A callback about the rear bed before Friday

The requested deadline concerns a callback, not an agreed site visit or completed work.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Front-path leaf clearance is complete. The rear-bed review was deferred by the lead, with no planting or diagnosis performed. Before Friday applies to the requested callback. It is not a booked visit date or a promise to complete work on the bed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
handover
Transfer of relevant work information and responsibility.give a clear handover
completed task
Defined work confirmed as finished.identify the completed task
deferred review
Assessment postponed rather than completed.record a deferred review
rear bed
Planting area at the back of the site.identify the rear bed
leaf removal
Clearing fallen leaves from a specified location.confirm front-path leaf removal
planting
Placing plants in a prepared location.distinguish planting from review
callback
Return telephone contact responding to a request.request a callback
callback deadline
Requested or agreed latest time for a return call.preserve the callback deadline
scheduling owner
Person responsible for arranging dates or contact.identify the scheduling owner
unbooked visit
Possible attendance without a confirmed appointment.describe an unbooked visit
status summary
Concise account of each relevant work stage.provide a status summary
pending item
Matter not yet resolved or finished.flag a pending item
lead decision
Direction made by the person supervising the work.record the lead decision
review outcome
Finding or decision resulting from an assessment.avoid inventing a review outcome
follow-up contact
Later communication about an unresolved matter.arrange follow-up contact
site attendance
Presence at the property for a stated purpose.distinguish site attendance from a callback
completion record
Note identifying work confirmed as finished.update the completion record
unperformed work
Task that has not been carried out.identify unperformed work
appointment status
Whether a visit is booked, proposed, or unresolved.confirm appointment status
customer priority
Matter the customer treats as important.preserve the customer priority
communication request
Request for information or contact.pass a communication request
open action
Next step not yet completed.assign an open action
ownership transfer
Passing responsibility for a defined follow-up.confirm ownership transfer
closeout
Final review or closure of a job or issue.avoid premature closeout

Ava owns scheduling, but that ownership does not mean an appointment already exists. The handoff should preserve the customer request and the pending arrangement. Avoid changing deferred into diagnosed, callback into site attendance, or front-path completion into whole-job closeout.

C Notice the language

Hand over separate statuses, not a single overall label

A visit may contain both completed work and an unresolved request. A short handoff still needs the distinction. Name what is finished, what was deferred, what the customer asked to hear, and who owns the next arrangement without declaring the whole visit complete or incomplete.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open with the completed item
The front-path leaves are cleared.
Name the pending area
The rear-bed review remains outstanding.
Explain the decision
The lead deferred that review.
State what was not done
No planting or diagnosis was carried out there.
Preserve the customer request
The customer wants a callback about the rear bed.
Give the requested deadline
The callback is requested before Friday.
Distinguish contact and attendance
That is a request for a call, not a booked visit.
Name the owner
Ava owns the scheduling follow-up.
State appointment status
No visit date is booked.
Avoid inventing an outcome
There is no review finding to report.
Separate the two areas
The front path is complete; the rear bed is a pending item.
Keep the request specific
The callback concerns the deferred rear-bed review.
Avoid a planting promise
No planting commitment was made during this visit.
Read back the action
Scheduling to follow up on the requested callback before Friday.
Preserve the unresolved detail
The date of any site attendance remains open.
Close the handoff
Please keep the completed task and the pending review as separate entries.
6 language and precision notes
Cleared
A completion statement limited to the front-path leaves.
Deferred
Postponed; it does not mean inspected, diagnosed, or permanently cancelled.
Before Friday
The requested callback deadline, not necessarily Friday itself.
Callback versus visit
Communication and physical attendance are different commitments.
Owns scheduling
Identifies responsibility without establishing an appointment.
Separate entries
Prevents one completed task from obscuring an unresolved item.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff preserves the two work statuses?

Answer and explanation

D. Front-path leaves cleared; rear-bed review deferred; no planting or diagnosis.

The handoff distinguishes the completed front task from the unperformed rear review.

2. What does before Friday refer to?

Answer and explanation

A. The requested callback

The customer requested communication before Friday, not an appointment or work-completion promise.

3. Which appointment statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Ava owns scheduling, but no visit date is booked.

Responsibility for scheduling does not establish that an appointment has been made.

4. Which closure would misstate the case?

Answer and explanation

D. Entire visit closed with all follow-up complete

A pending review and callback request remain, so all follow-up cannot be declared complete.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

A clear path and an open bed review

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Grounds worker / Ava: Office coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • appointment status
  • pending item
  • communication request
  • review outcome
  • closeout
  • scheduling owner
  • deferred review
  • completed task
  • open action
  • callback deadline
  1. Rosa
    Ava, Pine Court handover: the front-path leaves are cleared. The rear-bed review needs to stay open.
  2. Ava
    Let me separate the entries. The 1 is front-path leaf clearance. What is the current status of the rear-bed item, and what does the customer need from us?
  3. Rosa
    The lead deferred the rear review. No planting or diagnosis was done there; I've no finding to pass on.
  4. Ava
    I will record a 2, not a completed inspection. I will also state that no planting or diagnosis took place in that area.
  5. Rosa
    The customer wants a callback about the rear bed before Friday. Please keep that timing with the request.
  6. Ava
    I have the 3 as before Friday. Does the customer already have a booked visit, or is the request only for contact about the next step?
  7. Rosa
    No visit is booked. They want a call about the next step, not a confirmed attendance on Friday.
  8. Ava
    Then the 4 remains unbooked. I will not put Friday down as a confirmed attendance date just because it appears in the callback request.
  9. Rosa
    Please don't let the cleared path close the rear-bed item. They have different statuses.
  10. Ava
    I will keep a separate 5 for the rear bed. The cleared front path can be recorded accurately without closing the unresolved review.
  11. Rosa
    You'll take the scheduling follow-up, correct? I want the next reader to know who owns it.
  12. Ava
    Yes, I am the 6. That identifies responsibility, but it does not mean a date has already been arranged or a visit has been confirmed.
  13. Rosa
    Please keep deferred as the lead's decision. It wasn't a diagnosis that planting or treatment was needed.
  14. Ava
    I will not invent a 7. The note will describe the deferral and the absence of planting or diagnosis, rather than supply a reasoned finding we do not have.
  15. Rosa
    Could you read the timing back? Before Friday means the callback request, not completed bed work.
  16. Ava
    The 8 is a callback about the rear bed before Friday. There is no promise of planting, diagnosis, or completed work by that time.
  17. Rosa
    That's right. Any later visit still needs arranging; we've only passed the request for contact.
  18. Ava
    I will retain that as an 9. The record will name me as the owner and will not say the callback has already happened.
  19. Rosa
    Thanks. The path can be marked complete without making the unfinished rear review disappear.
  20. Ava
    Agreed. I will avoid premature 10. The handover will retain the completed front task, deferred rear review, requested callback deadline, and unbooked visit status as separate facts.

Answers and explanations
  1. completed task Turn 2. Completed task is front-path leaf clearance only; it does not include rear-bed assessment, planting, or diagnosis.
  2. deferred review Turn 4. Deferred review means the assessment was postponed, not completed with a finding or converted into planting work.
  3. callback deadline Turn 6. Callback deadline is the customer's request for contact before Friday; it is not a confirmed work or visit deadline.
  4. appointment status Turn 8. Appointment status remains unbooked; the callback request cannot be treated as confirmation of physical attendance.
  5. pending item Turn 10. Pending item is the rear-bed review and its follow-up, which must remain visible alongside the completed front task.
  6. scheduling owner Turn 12. Scheduling owner identifies Ava's responsibility for arrangements; it does not imply that she has already booked a date.
  7. review outcome Turn 14. Review outcome is absent because the rear-bed assessment was deferred and no diagnosis was performed.
  8. communication request Turn 16. Communication request is a callback about the rear bed before Friday, distinct from a booked visit or completed grounds work.
  9. open action Turn 18. Open action is the unresolved scheduling follow-up on the callback request; it is not evidence that contact has already happened.
  10. closeout Turn 20. Closeout of all work would be inaccurate because the rear-bed review and requested follow-up remain unresolved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: Ava, Pine Court handover: the front-path leaves are cleared. The rear-bed review needs to stay open.
  2. Ava: Let me separate the entries. The completed task is front-path leaf clearance. What is the current status of the rear-bed item, and what does the customer need from us?
  3. Rosa: The lead deferred the rear review. No planting or diagnosis was done there; I've no finding to pass on.
  4. Ava: I will record a deferred review, not a completed inspection. I will also state that no planting or diagnosis took place in that area.
  5. Rosa: The customer wants a callback about the rear bed before Friday. Please keep that timing with the request.
  6. Ava: I have the callback deadline as before Friday. Does the customer already have a booked visit, or is the request only for contact about the next step?
  7. Rosa: No visit is booked. They want a call about the next step, not a confirmed attendance on Friday.
  8. Ava: Then the appointment status remains unbooked. I will not put Friday down as a confirmed attendance date just because it appears in the callback request.
  9. Rosa: Please don't let the cleared path close the rear-bed item. They have different statuses.
  10. Ava: I will keep a separate pending item for the rear bed. The cleared front path can be recorded accurately without closing the unresolved review.
  11. Rosa: You'll take the scheduling follow-up, correct? I want the next reader to know who owns it.
  12. Ava: Yes, I am the scheduling owner. That identifies responsibility, but it does not mean a date has already been arranged or a visit has been confirmed.
  13. Rosa: Please keep deferred as the lead's decision. It wasn't a diagnosis that planting or treatment was needed.
  14. Ava: I will not invent a review outcome. The note will describe the deferral and the absence of planting or diagnosis, rather than supply a reasoned finding we do not have.
  15. Rosa: Could you read the timing back? Before Friday means the callback request, not completed bed work.
  16. Ava: The communication request is a callback about the rear bed before Friday. There is no promise of planting, diagnosis, or completed work by that time.
  17. Rosa: That's right. Any later visit still needs arranging; we've only passed the request for contact.
  18. Ava: I will retain that as an open action. The record will name me as the owner and will not say the callback has already happened.
  19. Rosa: Thanks. The path can be marked complete without making the unfinished rear review disappear.
  20. Ava: Agreed. I will avoid premature closeout. The handover will retain the completed front task, deferred rear review, requested callback deadline, and unbooked visit status as separate facts.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Front path complete, rear-bed review deferred

A worker hands Pine Court's unresolved request to office contact Ava.

  1. Worker: Ava, Pine Court's front-path clearance is complete; rear-bed review remains deferred.
  2. Office clerk: Was any planting or plant diagnosis carried out there, or should those remain unconfirmed?
  3. Worker: Neither was done. The customer requests a callback about that bed before Friday, not a confirmed return date.
  4. Office clerk: I'll take scheduling follow-up. Has a specific callback time already been agreed with the customer?
  5. Worker: No. Please confirm the contact arrangement with them rather than treating before Friday as a booked visit.
  6. Office clerk: Understood. I'll record the callback request separately from the outstanding site review.
  7. Worker: Please retain the front-path completion so the next crew doesn't repeat it.
  8. Office clerk: Yes. The completion note will retain both the finished task and the deferred item.
  9. Worker: Please acknowledge you're the follow-up owner so the customer's question doesn't remain with today's departing crew.
  10. Office clerk: I'll own the callback arrangement; the rear-bed follow-up visit remains unbooked.
A seasonal request in the wrong month

A worker and office clerk clarify a customer's future request.

  1. Worker: The autumn inquiry was mistakenly recorded as this week's unfinished work.
  2. Office clerk: Did the customer request work now, or information about the seasonal schedule later in the year?
  3. Worker: They asked when autumn visits could be discussed. No current appointment or additional work was requested.
  4. Office clerk: Then the note needs to identify a future scheduling inquiry, not unfinished work from today's visit.
  5. Worker: Exactly. Today's listed tasks are complete, and I don't want the next crew sent back for an unbooked task.
  6. Office clerk: Who should contact the customer about seasonal options, and is there an agreed response deadline?
  7. Worker: The office owns the inquiry; no response deadline was agreed.
  8. Office clerk: I'll correct the classification and place the inquiry with scheduling rather than today's completion follow-up.
  9. Worker: Please keep the customer's wording about autumn so the time period doesn't disappear in the shorter note.
  10. Office clerk: I'll retain autumn in the inquiry while keeping today's work complete.
The next crew needs the customer's exact limit

A departing worker hands a customer preference to an incoming colleague.

  1. Departing worker: The entrance-sign bed is excluded from the next confirmed visit.
  2. Incoming worker: Does that exclusion apply to all front planting, or only the bed around the entrance sign?
  3. Departing worker: Only the sign bed. The two long front beds remain on the next work order.
  4. Incoming worker: Thanks. If the handoff just says avoid the front bed, I'd have several possible interpretations.
  5. Departing worker: I'll use the sign as the landmark and the work-order wording for the other two beds.
  6. Incoming worker: Has the office updated the next booking, or are we passing on an unconfirmed customer preference?
  7. Departing worker: The office already confirmed that exclusion; this handoff highlights the booking.
  8. Incoming worker: Then I can check the same record rather than rely solely on an informal message from the previous crew.
  9. Departing worker: Exactly. No new planting method or material choice was agreed in that customer conversation.
  10. Incoming worker: I'll preserve the sign-bed exclusion and the two included front beds.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A clear path and an open bed review
  1. Read front path complete and rear-bed review deferred as two separate entries.
  2. Repeat before Friday as the requested callback timing, not a booked visit.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Hand over four distinct facts

Complete the office handoff. Preserve the finished front task, deferred rear review, requested callback timing, and absence of a booked visit.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The front-path leaves are ___."

Answer and explanation

A. cleared

Cleared confirms the completed front-path task, not all work at the site.

2. Coordinator: "The rear-bed review was ___ by the lead."

Answer and explanation

C. deferred

Deferred means postponed; no planting or diagnosis was performed there.

3. Worker: "The customer requests a callback before ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Friday

Friday belongs to the requested callback deadline, not a booked attendance date.

4. Coordinator: "I own scheduling, but no visit is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. booked

Scheduling responsibility exists, while the actual visit date remains unconfirmed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Landscaping and Grounds English
Professional audience: Landscape crew members, groundskeepers, lawn-service workers, and grounds assistants
Scope: All sites, customers, plants, materials, times, work orders, and decisions in the cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not horticultural diagnosis, pruning technique, equipment operation, chemical application, land surveying, or legal boundary advice. Follow actual site controls, training, work orders, local requirements, and qualified-lead decisions. The dialogues do not authorize entering disputed land, pruning a plant, changing irrigation, applying products, substituting materials, or resuming paused work.
Lesson 08: Handing over unfinished grounds work
Original fictional case: At Pine Court, worker Rosa hands the visit over to office coordinator Ava. Front-path leaves are cleared. The lead deferred the rear-bed review; no planting or diagnosis was done there. The customer asks for a callback about the rear bed before Friday. Ava owns scheduling, but no visit date is booked. Rosa must distinguish the completed front-path task, the deferred review, the requested communication deadline, and the unresolved appointment.
Language workshop: A clear path and an open bed review
Communication goal: Separate completed work from a deferred review, preserve a callback request, and identify who owns the next arrangement.
Language guidance: Front-path leaf clearance is complete. The rear-bed review was deferred by the lead, with no planting or diagnosis performed. Before Friday applies to the requested callback. It is not a booked visit date or a promise to complete work on the bed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The front-path leaves are cleared. / The rear-bed review remains outstanding. / The lead deferred that review. / No planting or diagnosis was carried out there. / The customer wants a callback about the rear bed. / The callback is requested before Friday. / That is a request for a call, not a booked visit. / Ava owns the scheduling follow-up. / No visit date is booked. / There is no review finding to report. / The front path is complete; the rear bed is a pending item. / The callback concerns the deferred rear-bed review. / No planting commitment was made during this visit. / Scheduling to follow up on the requested callback before Friday. / The date of any site attendance remains open. / Please keep the completed task and the pending review as separate entries.
Vocabulary: handover: Transfer of relevant work information and responsibility. / completed task: Defined work confirmed as finished. / deferred review: Assessment postponed rather than completed. / rear bed: Planting area at the back of the site. / leaf removal: Clearing fallen leaves from a specified location. / planting: Placing plants in a prepared location. / callback: Return telephone contact responding to a request. / callback deadline: Requested or agreed latest time for a return call. / scheduling owner: Person responsible for arranging dates or contact. / unbooked visit: Possible attendance without a confirmed appointment. / status summary: Concise account of each relevant work stage. / pending item: Matter not yet resolved or finished. / lead decision: Direction made by the person supervising the work. / review outcome: Finding or decision resulting from an assessment. / follow-up contact: Later communication about an unresolved matter. / site attendance: Presence at the property for a stated purpose. / completion record: Note identifying work confirmed as finished. / unperformed work: Task that has not been carried out. / appointment status: Whether a visit is booked, proposed, or unresolved. / customer priority: Matter the customer treats as important. / communication request: Request for information or contact. / open action: Next step not yet completed. / ownership transfer: Passing responsibility for a defined follow-up. / closeout: Final review or closure of a job or issue.
Speaking task: Read front path complete and rear-bed review deferred as two separate entries. Repeat before Friday as the requested callback timing, not a booked visit. Read Ava's scheduling ownership while leaving the actual appointment unbooked.
Partner: Rosa: Grounds worker / Ava: Office coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the office handoff. Preserve the finished front task, deferred rear review, requested callback timing, and absence of a booked visit.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: That's right. Any later visit still needs arranging; we've only passed the request for contact.
Ava: I will retain that as an open action. The record will name me as the owner and will not say the callback has already happened.
Rosa: Thanks. The path can be marked complete without making the unfinished rear review disappear.
Ava: Agreed. I will avoid premature closeout. The handover will retain the completed front task, deferred rear review, requested callback deadline, and unbooked visit status as separate facts.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Area is not the order volume

Read back area, specified depth, purchasing allowance, and whole-bag rounding as separate quantities.

Fictional estimator's worksheet: two non-overlapping rectangles, 12 m by 2 m and 4 m by 3 m, include 4 square meters of marked exclusions. The approved new layer is 50 mm. For purchasing only, add 10% to net volume and round up to whole 50-liter bags. This is supplied estimating arithmetic, not a general mulch-depth recommendation.

Dev: Grounds worker / Ava: Estimator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • round up
  • rounding surplus
  • allowance
  • net area
  • gross area
  • volume
  1. Dev
    Ava, the worksheet says thirty-six square meters. I was about to use that as the number of bags, but the units don't look right.
  2. Ava
    Start with the 1: twelve times two is twenty-four; four times three is twelve. Together they give thirty-six square meters before the exclusions.
  3. Dev
    The marked exclusions total four square meters and are inside those rectangles. So I subtract four once, leaving thirty-two to cover.
  4. Ava
    That's the 2. Keep the exclusions in the takeoff; don't buy or apply material as though every part of the rectangles needs coverage.
  5. Dev
    The new layer is specified as fifty millimeters. I need meters for multiplying by square meters, so that's zero point zero five meters.
  6. Ava
    Then the net 3 is thirty-two times zero point zero five: one point six cubic meters, or sixteen hundred liters.
  7. Dev
    At fifty liters per bag, the net material is thirty-two bags. But the purchasing instruction also asks for ten percent extra.
  8. Ava
    Apply the 4 to the net volume, not to the depth specification. Sixteen hundred plus one hundred sixty gives seventeen hundred sixty liters.
  9. Dev
    Dividing that by fifty gives thirty-five point two bags. We can't order point two of one of these bags.
  10. Ava
    So 5 to thirty-six whole bags. Thirty-five would give seventeen hundred fifty liters, ten liters below the purchasing target.
  11. Dev
    Thirty-six bags gives eighteen hundred liters. That's forty liters beyond the allowance-adjusted target, because of the bag increment.
  12. Ava
    Correct. That is 6, not another planned layer. Keep net requirement, allowance, and bag rounding separate in the estimate.
  13. Dev
    Could someone read thirty-six bags as an instruction to spread all thirty-six across the thirty-two square meters?
  14. Ava
    They shouldn't. Extra purchasing quantity doesn't change the approved fifty-millimeter new layer or remove the marked exclusions. Don't thicken the layer to consume leftovers.
  15. Dev
    And fifty millimeters is the specification for this fictional worksheet. It isn't a rule for every plant, site, or existing mulch condition.
  16. Ava
    Exactly. Actual depth and placement require the relevant design and site assessment. We're practicing how to explain the supplied calculation, not choosing horticultural requirements.
  17. Dev
    My estimate should therefore list thirty-two square meters net, one point six cubic meters net material, and one point seven six with allowance.
  18. Ava
    Then thirty-six fifty-liter bags as the rounded purchasing quantity. Show the bag volume so nobody substitutes a smaller bag at the same count.
  19. Dev
    I'll preserve the two rectangle dimensions and four-square-meter exclusions with it. That makes the total traceable if the drawing changes.
  20. Ava
    Good. This is a quantity calculation for review, not confirmation that material is ordered, delivered, or applied. Those statuses need their own records.

Answers and explanations
  1. gross area Turn 2. The two non-overlapping rectangles have areas twenty-four and twelve square meters, totaling thirty-six before exclusions.
  2. net area Turn 4. Thirty-six square meters minus four square meters of exclusions leaves thirty-two square meters requiring the new layer.
  3. volume Turn 6. Thirty-two square meters multiplied by 0.05 meters gives 1.6 cubic meters, equivalent to 1,600 liters.
  4. allowance Turn 8. Ten percent of the 1,600-liter net volume is 160 liters; the purchasing target becomes 1,760 liters.
  5. round up Turn 10. The 35.2-bag purchasing target requires thirty-six whole bags; rounding down to thirty-five would provide only 1,750 liters.
  6. rounding surplus Turn 12. The whole-bag order supplies 1,800 liters, forty above the 1,760-liter purchasing target; it is separate from the allowance itself.
